<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — UI, UX and why marketers should care

```text
Picture this. You spend five hundred dollars on an ad. People love it. They click. And then almost nobody signs up. The ad did its job. So what went wrong? Nine times out of ten, the answer is what happened after the click. That's user experience. In this lecture, you'll learn the difference between UI and UX, why UX is really a marketing problem, the mindset that separates good pages from expensive ones, and a twenty-minute quick scan you can run on any page today. By the end, you'll know exactly where to look when clicks don't turn into customers.
```

Scene 2 — Two terms, one experience

```text
Let's define two terms that often get mixed up. User experience, or UX, is the whole experience someone has when they try to get something done. Can they find what they need? Do they understand it? Do they trust it? Can they finish without frustration? User interface, or UI, is the part they see and touch. The layout, the buttons, the form fields, the colors, the little message that appears when they tap something. UI is a big part of UX. But UX also includes speed, content, pricing clarity, and even what happens after someone buys.
```

Scene 3 — The restaurant analogy

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a restaurant. The UI is the menu design, the table, the cutlery. The UX is everything. How long you waited for a table. Whether the waiter explained the dishes. Whether the bill had surprise charges. You can have a gorgeous menu and still leave angry. Websites work the same way. A beautiful page with a slow load, a confusing form, or a hidden delivery fee is a beautiful menu in a bad restaurant. And here's why it matters for marketers. Marketing buys attention. UX decides whether that attention turns into results.
```

Scene 4 — Where marketers touch UX

```text
So where do marketers touch UX? Almost everywhere. Landing pages. Sign-up forms. Emails and text messages. Website navigation. Checkout and booking flows. Link-in-bio pages. Even the chat widget in the corner. Each one raises a UX question. Does the page match the ad's promise? Are we asking for only the information we need? Can people find pricing in two taps? Improving these is often one of the most cost-effective ways to grow, because it raises the value of traffic you're already paying for. You don't need more visitors. You need more of your current visitors to succeed.
```

Scene 5 — You are not your user

```text
Now the most important idea in this whole course. You are not your user. You know your product, your jargon and where everything lives. Your visitors don't. They arrive distracted, on a small screen, often in a social app's built-in browser, with one question in their head. So three habits. First, start from the user's goal, not your message. Ask what this person is trying to do right now. Second, watch behavior, not opinions. What people do on a page beats what they say they like. Third, iterate. Treat every page as a hypothesis you can test and improve.
```

Scene 6 — Seven UX qualities

```text
To judge any experience, use seven qualities. Useful: does it meet a real need? Usable: can people complete the task easily? Findable: can they locate what they need? Credible: do they trust it? Accessible: can people with disabilities use it? Desirable: does it look and feel appealing and on brand? And fast: does it load and respond quickly, especially on mobile? Here's the key idea. If any one of these fails, the experience fails for someone. A page can be beautiful and useless. It can be useful and untrustworthy. You need all seven.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: webinar sign-up

```text
Worked example one. A simple one. A creator runs ads for a free LinkedIn growth webinar. Clicks are healthy, registrations are poor. Let's apply the qualities. The ad promises grow on LinkedIn in thirty days. The page headline says welcome to my community. That's weak message match. The form asks for seven fields, including company and job title. On mobile, the register button sits below a long biography. And a heavy background video slows the load. The fixes are straightforward. Repeat the ad's promise in the headline. Cut the form to name and email. Move the button up. Swap the video for a compressed image.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: cleaning company (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. A home cleaning company in Dubai spends on Instagram ads. The dashboard shows a two percent click-through rate but only half a percent of visitors book. The marketing lead suspects the ad. But a quick scan shows something else. Inside Instagram's in-app browser, the booking widget asks visitors to log in to a third-party account. Many don't have one. The price only appears after entering an address. And there's no WhatsApp option, which local customers expect. The fix wasn't a new ad. It was a guest booking option, a from price on the page, and a WhatsApp button.
```

Scene 9 — Watch me do it: the quick scan

```text
Watch me do it. I'll run the twenty-minute quick scan on a landing page. First, I open the real ad on my phone and tap through inside the app, because that's how customers arrive. I note it took about four seconds before I could see the headline and button. Next, a five-second test. I show the page to a colleague who's never seen it, then hide it. She says, it's something about cleaning, maybe a job listing? That's a red flag. Then I try to book with a test email and note two hesitations. Finally, I score the seven qualities and write one change to test.
```

Scene 10 — Measure before you change

```text
Before you change anything, capture a baseline. Pick one primary metric for the page, like bookings per visitor, and one guardrail metric that must not get worse, like refund requests or lead quality. Compare like with like: the same traffic source, similar days of the week, and enough time for random swings to settle. You can also use an AI assistant as a second reviewer. Paste in the ad and the page text, and ask it to list mismatches and the worries a cautious buyer might have. Treat that as a list of hypotheses. It can't see real behavior. Your users can.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Let's cover the common mistakes. Treating UX as making it pretty, when the real problems are clarity, speed and trust. Designing from the company's perspective, what we want to say, instead of the visitor's goal. Judging pages on a big desktop monitor when most social traffic is on phones. Testing only in a normal browser, never inside the social apps where your ads actually open. And changing lots of things at once based on opinions, then having no idea what worked. Every one of these is avoidable with the quick scan and a baseline.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. UX is the whole experience of reaching a goal. UI is the part people see and touch. Marketing buys attention, and UX decides whether it converts. Remember, you are not your user. Start from their goal, watch behavior, and iterate. Judge every experience on seven qualities: useful, usable, findable, credible, accessible, desirable and fast. Try this now. Pick your most important landing page or link-in-bio page. Open it from a real ad or post on your phone, run the quick scan template from the lesson, and write down one change and the metric it should move.
```

</details>

### 2. The 10 usability heuristics in practice

Lesson `usability-heuristics` · module “UX foundations for marketers” · target 9 min · 12 scenes · 1194 words · planned 8:37 (words ≈ 8:39) · status: **to produce**

| Term | Say |
|---|---|
| FAQ | F A Q |

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0:00 | 40 | Have you ever tapped a button, seen nothing happen, and tapped it again? And then discovered you'd ordered two of something? That tiny moment of confusion has a name, and a fix. In the nineteen nineties, usability expert Jakob Nielsen published ten general principles for interface design, called usability heuristics. Three decades later, they're still one of the fastest ways to find problems on any page. In this lecture, you'll learn all ten with marketing examples, how to run a quick heuristic review, how to score severity, and where this method falls short. | The 10 usability heuristics<br>• Tap, nothing happens, tap again<br>• Ten principles from Jakob Nielsen<br>• Run a review and score severity | Layout: phone mockup with a shopping cart. 0-10s: a finger taps 'Add to cart' twice; 10-20s: the cart badge jumps from zero to two; 20-30s: a red warning icon pulses; 30-40s: the title card and a list of ten numbered slots appear. |
| 2 | 0:40 | 55 | Why should a marketer care about a checklist from the nineties? Because heuristic reviews are fast and cheap. One person with a phone can review a landing page in thirty minutes and find the problems that are quietly wasting ad spend. No recruitment, no special tools. Think of heuristics like a pilot's pre-flight checklist. The pilot knows how to fly. The checklist makes sure nothing obvious gets missed when everyone's in a hurry. Before a campaign launch, you're in a hurry too. And because the ten heuristics are shared vocabulary, they also end arguments. Instead of saying I don't like this pop-up, you can say this breaks user control and freedom, because the close button is hard to find. That turns taste into a reasoned, fixable problem. | Why it matters<br>• Fast and cheap<br>• Finds problems wasting ad spend<br>• Like a pilot's pre-flight checklist | Layout: cockpit checklist morphing into a web page checklist. 0-14s: a clipboard with ticked items appears; 14-28s: a timer shows thirty minutes; 28-41s: the clipboard transforms into a landing page with ticks and crosses; 41-55s: caption: 'Before every launch'. |
| 3 | 1:35 | 45 | Let's go through the first five. One, visibility of system status. Keep people informed, like showing sending, then thanks, check your inbox, after a form submit. Two, match between the system and the real world. Use your customers' words. Book a call, not initiate consultation workflow. Three, user control and freedom. Give people a clear way to undo, go back or close, like a close button on a pop-up that's actually findable. Four, consistency and standards. Links look like links, and buttons look the same across the site. Five, error prevention. Stop mistakes before they happen, with a date picker instead of free text. | Heuristics 1 to 5<br>• 1 Visibility of system status<br>• 2 Match the real world<br>• 3 User control and freedom<br>• 4 Consistency • 5 Error prevention | Layout: numbered cards with mini examples. 0-11s: card one shows a form changing to 'Thanks, check your inbox'; 11-22s: card two swaps jargon for 'Book a call'; 22-34s: card three enlarges a tiny pop-up close button; 34-45s: cards four and five show matching buttons and a date picker. |
| 4 | 2:20 | 42 | Now six to ten. Six, recognition rather than recall. Show options instead of making people remember them, like displaying the chosen plan at checkout. Seven, flexibility and efficiency. Help newcomers and regulars, with autofill and saved details. Eight, aesthetic and minimalist design. Remove anything that competes with what matters, often one primary call to action per section. Nine, help users recognize, diagnose and recover from errors. Instead of error code fifty-one, say your card was declined, please try another card. And ten, help and documentation. Put answers where questions arise, like an FAQ right beside pricing. | Heuristics 6 to 10<br>• 6 Recognition over recall<br>• 7 Flexibility and efficiency<br>• 8 Minimalist design<br>• 9 Plain-language errors • 10 Help where needed | Layout: numbered cards continue. 0-10s: card six shows a checkout summary with the plan name; 10-21s: card seven shows autofill completing a form; 21-32s: card eight removes four competing buttons; 32-42s: cards nine and ten show a friendly error and an FAQ beside pricing. |
| 5 | 3:02 | 43 | Here's how to run a review. First, define one task, like register for the webinar on mobile. Second, walk through it as a new user would, on the device your audience actually uses. Third, log every problem: where it happens, which heuristic it breaks, and a suggested fix. Fourth, rate severity. A common scale runs from zero, not a problem, to four, blocks the task. Think about frequency, how many people hit it, impact, how badly it hurts, and persistence, whether it keeps happening. Fifth, if you can, have two or three people review independently. Different people spot different problems. | Running a heuristic review<br>• Define one task and device<br>• Walk it as a newcomer<br>• Log location, heuristic, fix<br>• Rate severity 0 to 4<br>• Two or three reviewers | Layout: spreadsheet building row by row. 0-11s: a task and device header appear; 11-22s: three rows fill with problems and heuristic tags; 22-32s: a severity column fills with numbers and color codes; 32-43s: two more reviewer sheets merge into one. |
| 6 | 3:45 | 43 | Worked example one. A small online store selling phone cases. The task: buy a case on mobile. After tapping add to cart, nothing visibly changes, so people tap again. That's heuristic one. Shipping is labeled fulfillment fee. That's heuristic two. Some buttons are green and rounded, others gray and square. Heuristic four. The phone model is typed as free text, causing wrong orders. Heuristic five. And card errors say transaction failed, code fifty-one. Heuristic nine. Each fix is small. A cart badge and a toast message. The word shipping. One button style. A model dropdown. A human error message. | Worked example 1: phone case store<br>• H1 no feedback after Add to cart<br>• H2 'fulfillment fee'<br>• H4 mixed button styles<br>• H5 free-text model • H9 error code | Layout: phone mockup with numbered annotations. 0-11s: annotation H1 appears on the unchanged cart; 11-22s: H2 highlights the jargon label; 22-32s: H4 and H5 highlight mismatched buttons and a text field; 32-43s: fixed versions slide over each problem. |
| 7 | 4:28 | 44 | Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. A digital agency in Manchester is about to launch a paid campaign for a client's online course. There are twenty-five findings from the review and one week to launch. What do they fix? They sort by severity. At the top: the enroll button is hidden behind a cookie banner on small phones, and the payment error just says failed. Both block the task for many people. Lower down: an inconsistent icon in the footer. They fix the top five before launch and schedule the rest. The campaign launches on a page that works. | Worked example 2: agency pre-launch (illustrative)<br>• 25 findings, one week<br>• Sort by severity<br>• Fix blockers first<br>• Schedule the rest | Layout: priority board with three columns. 0-11s: twenty-five sticky notes appear in a pile, tagged Illustrative; 11-22s: notes sort into 'Blocker', 'Serious', 'Minor' columns; 22-33s: the top five move to 'Before launch'; 33-44s: a calendar marks the launch date with a green tick. |
| 8 | 5:12 | 43 | Watch me do it. I open a shared sheet with columns for task, screen, problem, heuristic, frequency, impact, persistence, severity and fix. My task: buy a gift card on Android from a Facebook ad. I walk the task and log only what I see. Tapped buy, spinner never stopped. That's heuristic one. I rate frequency two, impact three, persistence two. My severity formula weights impact the most and gives me a four. Next row: the delivery date uses a free text box. Heuristic five, severity two. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — The 10 usability heuristics

```text
Have you ever tapped a button, seen nothing happen, and tapped it again? And then discovered you'd ordered two of something? That tiny moment of confusion has a name, and a fix. In the nineteen nineties, usability expert Jakob Nielsen published ten general principles for interface design, called usability heuristics. Three decades later, they're still one of the fastest ways to find problems on any page. In this lecture, you'll learn all ten with marketing examples, how to run a quick heuristic review, how to score severity, and where this method falls short.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why should a marketer care about a checklist from the nineties? Because heuristic reviews are fast and cheap. One person with a phone can review a landing page in thirty minutes and find the problems that are quietly wasting ad spend. No recruitment, no special tools. Think of heuristics like a pilot's pre-flight checklist. The pilot knows how to fly. The checklist makes sure nothing obvious gets missed when everyone's in a hurry. Before a campaign launch, you're in a hurry too. And because the ten heuristics are shared vocabulary, they also end arguments. Instead of saying I don't like this pop-up, you can say this breaks user control and freedom, because the close button is hard to find. That turns taste into a reasoned, fixable problem.
```

Scene 3 — Heuristics 1 to 5

```text
Let's go through the first five. One, visibility of system status. Keep people informed, like showing sending, then thanks, check your inbox, after a form submit. Two, match between the system and the real world. Use your customers' words. Book a call, not initiate consultation workflow. Three, user control and freedom. Give people a clear way to undo, go back or close, like a close button on a pop-up that's actually findable. Four, consistency and standards. Links look like links, and buttons look the same across the site. Five, error prevention. Stop mistakes before they happen, with a date picker instead of free text.
```

Scene 4 — Heuristics 6 to 10

```text
Now six to ten. Six, recognition rather than recall. Show options instead of making people remember them, like displaying the chosen plan at checkout. Seven, flexibility and efficiency. Help newcomers and regulars, with autofill and saved details. Eight, aesthetic and minimalist design. Remove anything that competes with what matters, often one primary call to action per section. Nine, help users recognize, diagnose and recover from errors. Instead of error code fifty-one, say your card was declined, please try another card. And ten, help and documentation. Put answers where questions arise, like an FAQ right beside pricing.
```

Scene 5 — Running a heuristic review

```text
Here's how to run a review. First, define one task, like register for the webinar on mobile. Second, walk through it as a new user would, on the device your audience actually uses. Third, log every problem: where it happens, which heuristic it breaks, and a suggested fix. Fourth, rate severity. A common scale runs from zero, not a problem, to four, blocks the task. Think about frequency, how many people hit it, impact, how badly it hurts, and persistence, whether it keeps happening. Fifth, if you can, have two or three people review independently. Different people spot different problems.
```

Scene 6 — Worked example 1: phone case store

```text
Worked example one. A small online store selling phone cases. The task: buy a case on mobile. After tapping add to cart, nothing visibly changes, so people tap again. That's heuristic one. Shipping is labeled fulfillment fee. That's heuristic two. Some buttons are green and rounded, others gray and square. Heuristic four. The phone model is typed as free text, causing wrong orders. Heuristic five. And card errors say transaction failed, code fifty-one. Heuristic nine. Each fix is small. A cart badge and a toast message. The word shipping. One button style. A model dropdown. A human error message.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 2: agency pre-launch (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. A digital agency in Manchester is about to launch a paid campaign for a client's online course. There are twenty-five findings from the review and one week to launch. What do they fix? They sort by severity. At the top: the enroll button is hidden behind a cookie banner on small phones, and the payment error just says failed. Both block the task for many people. Lower down: an inconsistent icon in the footer. They fix the top five before launch and schedule the rest. The campaign launches on a page that works.
```

Scene 8 — Watch me do it: the review sheet

```text
Watch me do it. I open a shared sheet with columns for task, screen, problem, heuristic, frequency, impact, persistence, severity and fix. My task: buy a gift card on Android from a Facebook ad. I walk the task and log only what I see. Tapped buy, spinner never stopped. That's heuristic one. I rate frequency two, impact three, persistence two. My severity formula weights impact the most and gives me a four. Next row: the delivery date uses a free text box. Heuristic five, severity two. After fifteen minutes, I sort by severity and highlight the top three fixes.
```

Scene 9 — AI and the limits of heuristics

```text
One more tool. You can paste screenshots into a multimodal AI assistant and ask it to suggest possible heuristic violations. It's good for breadth. It might notice jargon or a missing confirmation message. But it also invents problems and misses context, like how your customers actually talk. So treat it as an extra reviewer whose findings you confirm on a real device. And remember the biggest limitation of heuristic reviews in general. They're expert judgment, not user evidence. They miss problems specific to your audience, and they create some false alarms. Combine them with usability testing and analytics.
```

Scene 10 — Severity turns a list into a plan

```text
Let's get more precise about severity, because that's what turns a list into a plan. Ask three questions about each problem. How many people hit it? That's frequency. How badly does it hurt them? That's impact. And does it keep happening, or is it a one-time annoyance? That's persistence. In the lesson sheet, a simple formula weights impact the most and maps the result onto a zero to four scale. The exact weights matter less than agreeing on them before the review. Then sort. A missing confirmation on a paid-traffic form will almost always beat a slightly inconsistent footer icon.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Evaluating on desktop when your customers are on phones. Listing problems without severity, so the team fixes trivial things first. Writing opinions instead of observations. Treating the heuristics as rigid laws instead of guides. And skipping real user testing because we already did a heuristic review. How do you know your review worked? Track open high-severity issues over time, and watch whether the specific problems stop showing up in recordings, support tickets and test sessions.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Nielsen's ten heuristics give you a fast, structured way to review any page or flow. Define a task, walk it on the right device, log each problem with its heuristic, severity and fix, and prioritize by frequency and impact. Use AI as an extra reviewer, not a judge. And always validate with real users. Try this now. Pick one key task on your site, like signing up or booking. Walk it on your phone, log at least six problems in the review sheet from the lesson, and choose the top three to fix before your next campaign.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Understanding users

```text
Here's a question. Who is your landing page actually for? If your answer is everyone, you're in trouble. Pages built for everyone end up speaking to no one. The good news: you don't need a research department to understand your users. You probably already have hundreds of real customer words sitting in your inbox, your DMs and your reviews. In this lecture, you'll learn how to capture users' goals, tasks, context and worries, how to write jobs-to-be-done statements, how to build a quick proto-persona, and how to turn raw customer messages into design decisions in about an hour.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter so much? Because every design decision rests on assumptions about users. What they want. What they already know. What might stop them. Better assumptions, grounded in evidence, lead to better pages. Think of it like cooking for guests. You could cook your favorite dish. Or you could ask what they like, whether anyone's allergic, and how hungry they are. Same skills, very different dinner. Research is simply asking before you cook.
```

Scene 3 — Capture five things

```text
For each key audience, capture five things. Their goal: what they ultimately want, like get more clients for my salon. Their task: what they're trying to do on this page right now, like compare booking software prices. Their context: device, place, time pressure, language, connection. Their anxieties: what might stop them, like hidden fees or will this work in my country. And their knowledge: how familiar they are with your category and its jargon. Here's the key idea. The task on this page is usually much smaller than the goal. Design for the task, and connect it to the goal.
```

Scene 4 — Jobs to be done

```text
Now jobs to be done. This frames needs as progress someone wants to make. The pattern is: when, some situation, I want to, some motivation, so I can, some outcome. For example. When I'm launching a new product, I want to get a professional landing page live in a day, so I can take pre-orders before my competitors. Notice what that tells you. This person needs speed, templates and payment setup. They do not need a long company history. Job statements help you decide what goes on the page and what doesn't.
```

Scene 5 — Worked example 1: proto-persona

```text
Worked example one, simple. A salon software company builds a proto-persona. That's a quick, assumption-based profile you validate over time. Label: busy salon owner. Context: runs a three-chair salon in Lahore and manages bookings on WhatsApp. Goal: fewer no-shows and less admin. Tasks on the site: understand features, check the price in local currency, see if it works with WhatsApp. Anxieties: cost, learning curve, whether staff will adopt it. Evidence: three sales calls and twenty support chats. And every line is marked as either assumed or evidenced. That honesty is what makes it useful.
```

Scene 6 — Worked example 2: coaching program (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. A coaching business in Toronto sells a twelve-week program. The team runs six short interviews and reviews fifty DM questions. Four themes appear. Price uncertainty. Time commitment. Is this for beginners? And payment options in local currency. So they turn each theme into a design implication. A who this is for, and not for, section. Weekly time commitment stated in hours. Prices shown clearly with installment terms. And an FAQ that uses the exact words people used in their DMs. Pre-sale questions about time and fees drop over the following month.
```

Scene 7 — Watch me do it: one-hour synthesis

```text
Watch me do it. I export forty recent DMs and support emails and remove names, phone numbers and order numbers. I paste each one onto a card in a whiteboard tool. For fifteen minutes I cluster silently. No labels yet. Then I name each cluster in the customer's voice. For example: I don't know if this works with WhatsApp. For the three biggest clusters, I fill in an insight card: the insight, two exact quotes, a design implication, where it goes on the site, and a metric to watch. The whole thing takes about an hour.
```

Scene 8 — AI-assisted synthesis, safely

```text
You can speed up that first clustering with an AI assistant. Paste your anonymized messages and ask it to group them into four to seven themes, with counts and two verbatim quotes each. Then check every quote against the originals. If a quote doesn't exist word for word, the theme is suspect, so redo it by hand. And a privacy rule: only paste anonymized data, into a tool your organization has approved, and check whether your inputs may be used for training. Follow the data protection laws that apply to your customers.
```

Scene 9 — Lightweight research sources

```text
Where do you find users if you have no budget? Start with what you already own. Support tickets, chats, DMs and reviews show real problems in customers' own words. Sales call notes reveal objections and decision criteria. A one-question on-site survey, like what's stopping you from booking today, tells you what's missing. Site search terms show what people look for and can't find. Analytics show what people do and where they drop off. And short interviews with five to eight customers give you motivations and language. Most of these cost nothing but an afternoon. Combine at least two sources before you trust a theme.
```

Scene 10 — Interview essentials

```text
A quick word on interviews. Ask about past behavior, not the future. Tell me about the last time you booked a salon appointment beats would you use an app for that? People are poor at predicting their own behavior. Ask open questions, follow up with why, and tell me more. Avoid leading questions like don't you think this is easy? Record only with consent, pay people fairly for their time, and never turn an interview into a disguised sales pitch.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Designing for everyone. Personas built only from demographics, with no goals or worries. Asking people to predict what they'd do. Letting AI invent themes that no customer actually said. And the most common of all: doing research, then ignoring it. Research only matters if it changes something. So for every design implication you ship, name the metric it should move, like fewer questions about fees, and check it a few weeks later.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Capture goals, tasks, context, anxieties and knowledge for each key audience. Use jobs-to-be-done statements to decide what belongs on the page. Start with proto-personas and mark what's assumed. Mine the messages you already have, cluster them, and turn themes into design implications. Use AI for a first pass, verify every quote, and protect privacy. Try this now. Export twenty recent customer messages, reviews or support tickets. Anonymize them, cluster them into themes, and write three insight cards, each with one design implication and one metric to watch.
```

</details>

### 4. User flows and journey maps

Lesson `user-flows-and-journeys` · module “Flows, structure and interface copy” · target 8 min · 12 scenes · 1132 words · planned 8:13 (words ≈ 8:12) · status: **to produce**

| Term | Say |
|---|---|
| GA4 | G A four |
| Riyadh | ree-YAHD |

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — User flows and journey maps

```text
Here's something marketers often miss. Nobody experiences your landing page on its own. They experience a path. An ad, a page, a form, a confirmation email, a reminder, maybe a WhatsApp message. And problems love to hide between the pages, where nobody on the team owns them. In this lecture, you'll learn to map user flows and customer journey maps, how to design for the edge cases real people hit, how to attach analytics numbers to every step, and how to simplify a flow so more people reach the finish line.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because every extra step, every channel switch and every unclear moment leaks people. Think of a flow like a garden hose with small holes. Each hole on its own looks harmless. But by the end of the hose, only a trickle comes out. Most teams try to fix this by pushing more water in, meaning more ad spend. Mapping the flow shows you where the holes are, so you can patch them instead. That's usually far cheaper than buying more traffic.
```

Scene 3 — User flows

```text
First, user flows. A user flow is a diagram of the steps someone takes to complete one task, including decisions and alternative paths. The notation is simple. Entry points and goals in square boxes. Pages or screens in rounded boxes. Decisions in diamonds, with arrows for yes and no. When you look at a flow, check five things. How many steps are there, and can any be removed or combined? Are there dead ends with no next step? What happens on error? Where does the user switch channels, like app to browser or email to WhatsApp? And do new and returning users take different paths?
```

Scene 4 — Happy path vs reality

```text
Next, the happy path versus reality. The happy path is the ideal route where nothing goes wrong. Real people take many other routes. Their payment is declined. They mistype their email. They open your link inside Instagram, then try to finish on a laptop. They use a screen reader or only a keyboard. Their connection drops mid-submit. Here's the key idea. You don't need to design every possible edge case. But you must design the common ones deliberately, because those are where real customers quietly give up.
```

Scene 5 — Customer journey maps

```text
Now zoom out. A customer journey map shows the whole relationship, not just one task. Columns are stages, like aware, consider, sign up, onboard and renew. Rows capture what people do, where they do it, what they think, how they feel, and your opportunities. For example, at the sign-up stage someone might think, is this safe? and feel anxious. The opportunity is a short form and clear trust signals. Journey maps are great for getting marketing, sales, product and support to see their part of the same experience.
```

Scene 6 — Worked example 1: webinar flow

```text
Worked example one, simple. A webinar from an Instagram story ad. The flow: ad, landing page, a two-field form, a thank-you page with a calendar link, a confirmation email, then reminders one day and one hour before. Walking it on a phone, we spot two problems. There's no error path, so a mistyped email just fails silently. And the calendar link opens a blank page inside Instagram's in-app browser. The fixes: inline error messages that say exactly what to correct, and a calendar option that works in in-app browsers, plus the link again in the confirmation email.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 2: consultancy (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. A consultancy in Riyadh runs Facebook ads for free strategy calls. The original flow: ad to homepage, then services page, contact page, a ten-field form, and a message saying we'll get back to you. Then days of email back and forth to find a time. Mapping it with analytics shows most mobile visitors never leave the homepage. The redesign: ad to a dedicated page that matches the ad, an embedded scheduler showing times in the visitor's time zone, a short form with one qualifying question, an instant confirmation and calendar invite, and reminders by email or text with consent.
```

Scene 8 — Watch me do it: flow + funnel

```text
Watch me do it. I pick one task: register for a webinar from Instagram. I write the flow as a text diagram in Mermaid syntax, which renders in many tools, so it's easy to share and update. Then I open GA4, go to Explore, and create a funnel exploration. Step one, page view on the webinar page. Step two, form start. Step three, generate lead. I break it down by device and filter to Instagram traffic. I write each step's numbers onto the diagram. The biggest relative drop is between form start and generate lead on mobile. That's where I'll look at recordings first.
```

Scene 9 — Make the map trustworthy

```text
A few practical notes on that walkthrough. The funnel is only as good as your tracking. Make sure each event actually fires, and that your analytics respect consent rules, before you trust the numbers. Keep a friction log beside the diagram, with each step, what the user sees, the friction you observed, the evidence, a fix idea, and the effort. And use the edge-case checklist from the lesson. Card declined. Mistyped email. Device switch. In-app browser. Keyboard and screen-reader users. Slow connections. Right-to-left language versions. Five minutes with that list prevents weeks of silent losses.
```

Scene 10 — Measure to the real finish line

```text
Let's talk about the finish line, because many teams stop measuring too early. A webinar registration isn't the real goal. Attendance is. A trial sign-up isn't the goal. Activation is. So when you map a flow, include the steps after the conversion: the confirmation, the reminders, the first login, the first use. Then record a baseline for the whole path before you change anything. After the change, compare the same traffic sources over a similar period, split by device. If registrations rise but attendance falls, you haven't improved the flow. You've just moved the leak further down the hose.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Designing screens in isolation, one page at a time. Ignoring error paths, so failures are silent. Forgetting what happens after conversion, like confirmation, onboarding, and the reminder that gets people to actually show up. Mapping the journey the company wishes existed, not the real one. And measuring only one page instead of the whole path. The right success metric is end-to-end completion, like ad click to confirmed booking, split by device and source. If that number improves, the flow improved.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. People experience paths, not pages. User flows map the steps, decisions, errors and channel switches for one task. Journey maps capture stages, thoughts, feelings and touchpoints across the whole relationship. Design the common edge cases on purpose, attach real funnel numbers, and measure end-to-end completion. Try this now. Take one of your ads or posts and map the full flow to conversion, including at least two edge cases. Add numbers from your analytics if you have them, mark every friction point, and draw a simplified version with fewer steps.
```

</details>

| 1 | 0:00 | 41 | Here's a real conversation that happens every week in small agencies. A prospect emails: what do you charge? And someone replies, it's on the website, under Investment. The prospect never found it. Not because they're lazy, but because nobody calls prices investment except the agency. That's an information architecture problem. In this lecture, you'll learn what information architecture is, how to label navigation in your users' words, how card sorting and tree testing work, and how to structure landing pages and multilingual sites so people find what they need in one or two taps. | Information architecture and navigation<br>• 'It's under Investment'<br>• Labels in users' words<br>• Card sorts and tree tests<br>• Structure for landing pages and languages | Layout: email exchange then a website menu. 0-10s: an email bubble asks 'What do you charge?'; 10-20s: a reply points to a menu item labeled 'Investment'; 20-31s: a search spotlight sweeps the menu and misses it; 31-41s: the label rewrites itself to 'Pricing' as the title appears. |
| 2 | 0:41 | 42 | Information architecture, or IA, is the structure and labeling of content so people can find what they need and understand where they are. It has five parts. Organization: how content is grouped, by service, audience or task. Labeling: what groups and links are called. Navigation: how people move around, with header menus, footers and breadcrumbs. Search: finding things by typing. And hierarchy: the levels, like home, then services, then one service. Poor IA shows up as high bounce rates, lots of support questions the website already answers, and prospects who say they couldn't find your prices. | What IA is<br>• Organization • Labeling • Navigation<br>• Search • Hierarchy<br>• Symptoms: bounces, repeat questions | Layout: five building blocks stacking into a house. 0-10s: five labeled blocks appear; 10-21s: they stack to form a house shape labeled 'Website'; 21-32s: symptom icons appear around a cracked version; 32-42s: the cracks close as blocks align. |
| 3 | 1:23 | 40 | Here's an analogy. Think about a supermarket. You walk in wanting rice. You don't know the store, but you find it in under a minute. Why? Clear signs above the aisles, in words you use, like rice and pasta, not carbohydrate solutions. Related things are grouped together. And the layout is similar to other supermarkets you've visited. A website works the same way. Clear signs are your navigation labels. Grouping is your organization. And familiarity is following conventions people already know, like pricing in the header and contact at the end. | The supermarket analogy<br>• Aisle signs = navigation labels<br>• Grouped shelves = organization<br>• Familiar layout = conventions | Layout: supermarket aisle illustration. 0-10s: overhead aisle signs appear: 'Rice and pasta', 'Dairy'; 10-20s: a joke sign 'Carbohydrate solutions' appears and gets crossed out; 20-30s: the aisles morph into a website header menu; 30-40s: caption: 'Signs in your customers' words'. |
| 4 | 2:03 | 43 | Labels are one of the biggest findability factors. Use the words your users use, from interviews, search queries and support tickets. Not internal jargon. Not clever branding. Solutions becomes Services. Investment becomes Pricing. Our Journey becomes About us. Resources Hub becomes Guides or Blog. Connect becomes Contact. Creative language can live in your content and headlines. But your navigation is a sign system, and signs must be instantly clear. For small business sites, four to seven top-level items is a common, manageable range, with the most important tasks first and one primary call to action in the header. | Labels in users' words<br>• Solutions → Services<br>• Investment → Pricing<br>• Our Journey → About us<br>• 4 to 7 top-level items + one CTA | Layout: label swap animation on a header menu. 0-11s: a header shows five vague labels; 11-22s: each label flips to its clear version one by one; 22-32s: a 'Book a call' button appears on the right; 32-43s: a counter shows six top-level items. |
| 5 | 2:46 | 43 | How do you know how users group things? Card sorting. In an open card sort, people group topics and name the groups themselves. That reveals their mental model and their words. In a closed card sort, they place topics into categories you've already defined, which tests your structure. Then there's tree testing. You show a text-only menu, no visual design, and give tasks like, you want to know what a new logo would cost, where would you click? If people can find it in the bare tree, your structure works. If not, no amount of visual polish will save it. | Card sorting and tree testing<br>• Open sort: users group and name<br>• Closed sort: users fill your categories<br>• Tree test: find items in a text-only menu | Layout: cards and a text tree side by side. 0-11s: topic cards shuffle into user-made groups with handwritten labels; 11-22s: the same cards drop into predefined boxes; 22-32s: a plain text menu tree appears; 32-43s: a cursor path traces a task to the correct item with a green tick. |
| 6 | 3:29 | 42 | Worked example one, simple. An agency website has nine menu items with creative labels: The Lab, Our DNA, Let's Talk Magic. Prospects can't find pricing or examples. The team reviews site search terms and sales call questions, and people keep saying price, cost, examples and portfolio. So they run an open card sort with eight clients, then tree-test a new structure. Services. Work. Pricing. About. Blog. Plus a Book a call button. And case studies filterable by industry. Over the next quarter, more visitors reach pricing and case studies, and the what do you charge emails drop. | Worked example 1: agency menu<br>• Nine creative labels<br>• Evidence from search and sales calls<br>• Card sort + tree test<br>• Five clear items + Book a call | Layout: before-and-after menus. 0-10s: a cluttered nine-item menu appears with creative labels; 10-21s: quotes from sales calls float up: 'price', 'examples'; 21-32s: the menu rebuilds as five clear items and a button; 32-42s: a small chart shows pricing-page visits rising. |
| 7 | 4:11 | 42 | Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. A furniture retailer in Dubai runs its site in English and Arabic. Arabic visitors browse fewer pages and use site search far more. A tree test with six Arabic-speaking customers reveals why. The Arabic menu was a literal translation, with categories organized differently from the English site, and the language switcher was a tiny flag icon. The fixes: an Arabic structure mirrored for right to left reading, category names chosen by Arabic-speaking customers, identical structure across both languages, and a switcher labeled in each language's own script. | Worked example 2: bilingual retailer (illustrative)<br>• Arabic visitors rely on search<br>• Literal translation, mismatched structure<br>• Tiny flag switcher<br>• Mirrored RTL, native labels, same structure | Layout: two menus side by side, English and Arabic. 0-10s: the Arabic menu appears out of order, tagged Illustrative; 10-21s: a search-usage bar for Arabic visitors towers over English; 21-32s: the menu mirrors right to left with new labels; 32-42s: a switcher reads 'English' and the word Arabic in Arabic script. |

| 9 | 5:37 | 44 | Two special cases. First, campaign landing pages. Many remove or reduce the main navigation to keep focus on one goal. That can help conversion, but keep essentials: the logo link, a minimal footer, and access to pricing, privacy and contact information so trust doesn't suffer. Second, multilingual sites. Provide an obvious language switcher with language names in their own script, mirror layouts for right to left languages, and keep the same structure across languages so people can switch without getting lost. And a bonus: clear labels and descriptive page titles also help search engines and AI answer engines understand your pages. | Two special cases<br>• Landing pages: less nav, keep essentials<br>• Multilingual: clear switcher, mirrored RTL<br>• Same structure across languages<br>• Clear labels help search and AI too | Layout: two panels. 0-11s: a landing page shows a stripped header with only a logo; 11-22s: a minimal footer appears with pricing, privacy and contact links; 22-33s: a bilingual site mirrors its layout; 33-44s: a search icon reads the clear page titles. |

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Information architecture and navigation

```text
Here's a real conversation that happens every week in small agencies. A prospect emails: what do you charge? And someone replies, it's on the website, under Investment. The prospect never found it. Not because they're lazy, but because nobody calls prices investment except the agency. That's an information architecture problem. In this lecture, you'll learn what information architecture is, how to label navigation in your users' words, how card sorting and tree testing work, and how to structure landing pages and multilingual sites so people find what they need in one or two taps.
```

Scene 2 — What IA is

```text
Information architecture, or IA, is the structure and labeling of content so people can find what they need and understand where they are. It has five parts. Organization: how content is grouped, by service, audience or task. Labeling: what groups and links are called. Navigation: how people move around, with header menus, footers and breadcrumbs. Search: finding things by typing. And hierarchy: the levels, like home, then services, then one service. Poor IA shows up as high bounce rates, lots of support questions the website already answers, and prospects who say they couldn't find your prices.
```

Scene 3 — The supermarket analogy

```text
Here's an analogy. Think about a supermarket. You walk in wanting rice. You don't know the store, but you find it in under a minute. Why? Clear signs above the aisles, in words you use, like rice and pasta, not carbohydrate solutions. Related things are grouped together. And the layout is similar to other supermarkets you've visited. A website works the same way. Clear signs are your navigation labels. Grouping is your organization. And familiarity is following conventions people already know, like pricing in the header and contact at the end.
```

Scene 4 — Labels in users' words

```text
Labels are one of the biggest findability factors. Use the words your users use, from interviews, search queries and support tickets. Not internal jargon. Not clever branding. Solutions becomes Services. Investment becomes Pricing. Our Journey becomes About us. Resources Hub becomes Guides or Blog. Connect becomes Contact. Creative language can live in your content and headlines. But your navigation is a sign system, and signs must be instantly clear. For small business sites, four to seven top-level items is a common, manageable range, with the most important tasks first and one primary call to action in the header.
```

Scene 5 — Card sorting and tree testing

```text
How do you know how users group things? Card sorting. In an open card sort, people group topics and name the groups themselves. That reveals their mental model and their words. In a closed card sort, they place topics into categories you've already defined, which tests your structure. Then there's tree testing. You show a text-only menu, no visual design, and give tasks like, you want to know what a new logo would cost, where would you click? If people can find it in the bare tree, your structure works. If not, no amount of visual polish will save it.
```

Scene 6 — Worked example 1: agency menu

```text
Worked example one, simple. An agency website has nine menu items with creative labels: The Lab, Our DNA, Let's Talk Magic. Prospects can't find pricing or examples. The team reviews site search terms and sales call questions, and people keep saying price, cost, examples and portfolio. So they run an open card sort with eight clients, then tree-test a new structure. Services. Work. Pricing. About. Blog. Plus a Book a call button. And case studies filterable by industry. Over the next quarter, more visitors reach pricing and case studies, and the what do you charge emails drop.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 2: bilingual retailer (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. A furniture retailer in Dubai runs its site in English and Arabic. Arabic visitors browse fewer pages and use site search far more. A tree test with six Arabic-speaking customers reveals why. The Arabic menu was a literal translation, with categories organized differently from the English site, and the language switcher was a tiny flag icon. The fixes: an Arabic structure mirrored for right to left reading, category names chosen by Arabic-speaking customers, identical structure across both languages, and a switcher labeled in each language's own script.
```

Scene 8 — Watch me do it: label test

```text
Watch me do it. First I collect words. In GA4, I check site search terms, and I pull the top questions from sales calls. Then I write two candidate structures in a spreadsheet. Next, a quick tree test with five people over video. I show only the text menu and give three tasks that don't reuse the menu words. For each task I record the first click, the final answer, and whether it was right. Task one, find logo pricing: two out of five succeed. That's a fail. I rename Investment to Pricing and re-test that one task. Five out of five.
```

Scene 9 — Two special cases

```text
Two special cases. First, campaign landing pages. Many remove or reduce the main navigation to keep focus on one goal. That can help conversion, but keep essentials: the logo link, a minimal footer, and access to pricing, privacy and contact information so trust doesn't suffer. Second, multilingual sites. Provide an obvious language switcher with language names in their own script, mirror layouts for right to left languages, and keep the same structure across languages so people can switch without getting lost. And a bonus: clear labels and descriptive page titles also help search engines and AI answer engines understand your pages.
```

Scene 10 — Mobile navigation

```text
A quick word on mobile navigation, because that's where most social traffic lands. The menu usually collapses behind an icon. Make sure that icon is recognizable, and consider adding the word menu next to it. Keep your primary call to action visible outside the menu, like a book a call button in the header, so people don't have to hunt for it. Put the most common tasks first inside the menu. Use breadcrumbs on deeper sites like shops and help centers. And add site search once you have a big content library. Then test it on a real phone, with your thumb, not a mouse.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Clever labels that hide meaning. Too many top-level items. Organizing by your internal team structure, like marketing department pages, instead of by user tasks. Hiding essential information in footers or PDFs. And skipping validation, because the structure seems obvious to the team. Remember, you're not your user. How do you measure success? Tree-test success rates. More homepage visitors reaching key pages like pricing and case studies. Less site search for things that are already in the menu. And fewer where can I find your prices questions.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Information architecture is how content is organized and labeled so people can find it. Use your users' words for labels, keep navigation shallow and focused, validate structures with card sorts and tree tests, and handle landing pages and multilingual sites deliberately. Try this now. Draw a simple sitemap for your website or a client's. Rewrite any unclear navigation labels using words from real customer messages and search terms. Then ask three people to find two items using only the text menu, and record how many succeed on the first try.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — UX writing, microcopy and forms

```text
Here's the cheapest conversion improvement you'll ever make. Changing words. No redesign. No developer. No new budget. Just the text on buttons, labels, hints and error messages. That text is called microcopy, and it quietly decides whether people finish your forms. In this lecture, you'll learn the principles of UX writing, how to write buttons that say what happens, how to design forms that ask less and explain more, how to write error messages that help instead of blame, and how to use AI to draft options you then edit and test.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter so much? Forms are where many conversions die. Someone has already decided to act. They've clicked your ad, read your page, and started typing. And then a vague button, a confusing field or a blaming error message makes them quit. Here's an analogy. Microcopy is like the signs in an airport. Nobody visits an airport for the signs. But when they're unclear, you miss your flight. When they're clear, you barely notice them, and you get where you're going.
```

Scene 3 — Six principles

```text
Six principles of UX writing. One, clear before clever. Say exactly what happens. Two, concise. Remove words that don't help people act. Three, useful. Answer the question someone has at that moment. Four, consistent. Use the same term for the same thing everywhere. If it's sign in on one page, don't make it log in on the next. Five, human. Match your brand voice, but never at the cost of clarity. And six, inclusive. Plain language, no idioms that don't translate, and never blame the user.
```

Scene 4 — Buttons that say what happens

```text
Buttons should describe the action or the outcome. Submit tells people nothing. Get my free guide tells them what they'll receive. Click here becomes download the checklist. Continue becomes continue to payment. OK on a dangerous action becomes delete project. And learn more, used everywhere, becomes see pricing or read the case study. Use verbs. And where there's risk, clarify the commitment near the button, like start free trial, no card needed. But only if it's true. Honesty in microcopy is part of trust, and in many markets it's also a consumer protection issue.
```

Scene 5 — Forms: ask less, explain more

```text
Now forms. Ask only what you need right now. Every field adds effort, so collect extra details later if you truly need them. Explain why for sensitive fields, like we'll only use your phone number for appointment reminders. Keep labels visible above fields, because placeholder text disappears when people type and often has poor contrast. Use the right input types, so phones show an email keyboard or a number pad. Support autofill. Use one column on mobile. And for marketing consent, use a clear, unticked opt-in checkbox with a link to your privacy information, in line with data protection laws.
```

Scene 6 — Errors that help

```text
Error messages follow a simple formula. What happened, plus how to fix it. Your card was declined. Please check the details or try another card. That email address is missing an at sign. Please check and try again. Good errors are specific, helpful, and shown right next to the field. They're polite, so no you entered an invalid value. And they're accessible. Don't rely only on red color. Include text, ideally an icon, and make sure screen readers announce the error. The same care goes into empty states, confirmations and loading messages.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: newsletter form

```text
Worked example one, simple. A newsletter sign-up. Before: a headline that says subscribe, six fields including date of birth and city, a pre-ticked box for partner offers, a submit button, and an error that says invalid input. After: a headline that says get one practical marketing tip every Tuesday. Two fields, first name optional and email. The partner box removed entirely. A button that says send me the tips. An error that says please enter an email address like name at example dot com. And a confirmation: almost done, check your inbox to confirm.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: clinic form (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. A health clinic in Karachi takes appointment requests online. The form has nine fields, placeholder-only labels, and a phone field that rejects numbers written with the country code. Form analytics show the phone field causes more abandonment than any other. The team rewrites the hint: enter your mobile number, for example zero three hundred, or with plus nine two. They accept both formats, add a visible label, explain that the number is only for appointment reminders, and cut three optional fields. Phone field errors fall sharply over the next month.
```

Scene 9 — Watch me do it: AI drafts, human edits

```text
Watch me do it with AI. I open an assistant and give it context. Product: a weekly tips newsletter for small business owners in the UAE and Pakistan. Then constraints. Five options each for the headline, the button, the missing at sign error and the success message. Plain English, buttons under eight words, no exclamation marks in errors, no blame, no promises we can't verify. It returns a table. I pick the strongest options and edit them against our style sheet. I change log in to sign in for consistency. Then I check the form code: visible labels, email input type, autocomplete, and an error linked to its field.
```

Scene 10 — Keep it consistent at scale

```text
Two habits keep microcopy good as your team grows. First, a microcopy style sheet. It's a short document listing the terms you use, like sign in not log in, cart not basket, plan not package. It sets your capitalization rule for buttons, how you write dates, times and prices in each market, and a few approved examples of errors and confirmations. Share it with everyone who writes, including freelancers and translators. Second, localization, not literal translation. Write microcopy for each language. Watch for text that grows longer in some languages, and make sure buttons can stretch without breaking, including in Arabic and Urdu layouts.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Generic buttons like submit and click here. Placeholder-only labels. Asking for personal data you don't need. Pre-ticked consent boxes and vague privacy statements. Error messages that blame or confuse. And shipping AI-written copy without review, especially for pricing, legal or consent text. How do you measure success? Track form start rate, completion rate and errors per field. Good microcopy lowers errors and abandonment on the fields you changed. And watch lead quality, so a shorter form doesn't just bring in more unqualified sign-ups.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Treat words as interface. Write clear, concise, consistent microcopy. Make buttons describe the action. Ask only what you need, keep labels visible, use the right keyboards and autofill, and make consent clear and unticked. Write errors that say what happened and how to fix it, without relying on color alone. Use AI for drafts, and humans for decisions. Try this now. Audit one form you own. Remove any unnecessary field. Rewrite the button, the labels and at least two error messages using the formula. And check that consent is clear, specific and unticked.
```

</details>

| 1 | 0:00 | 43 | Have you ever sat in a meeting where everyone argued about the color of a button, on a page whose structure was completely wrong? It happens all the time. And it's expensive, because by the time someone notices the structure problem, the page is already designed, built and paid for. Wireframes prevent that. In this lecture, you'll learn what wireframes are, the fidelity levels and when to use each, how to start from a content priority list, why mobile comes first, how to annotate behavior, and how to build a clean wireframe in Figma in about twenty minutes. | Sketching and wireframing<br>• Arguing about colors on the wrong structure<br>• Fidelity levels<br>• Content first, mobile first<br>• Annotate and review | Layout: meeting table with a polished page on screen. 0-11s: speech bubbles argue 'Blue?' 'Green?'; 11-22s: the page's sections shuffle to reveal a structural flaw; 22-32s: a price tag labeled 'rework' appears; 32-43s: the page fades to a gray wireframe as the title appears. |
| 2 | 0:43 | 38 | A wireframe is a simplified layout that shows structure, content priority and function, without final colors, images or polished typography. Why bother? Because wireframes let you agree on what goes where, and why, before anyone spends time on visuals or code. Here's an analogy. Before building a house, an architect draws a floor plan. Nobody chooses curtains from a floor plan. You decide where the kitchen goes, whether the bedrooms get light, and whether the front door leads somewhere sensible. A wireframe is your page's floor plan. | What a wireframe is<br>• Structure, priority, function<br>• No final colors or images<br>• The floor plan of your page | Layout: architect's floor plan morphing into a wireframe. 0-10s: a floor plan draws with labeled rooms; 10-19s: curtain swatches try to attach and bounce off; 19-28s: the plan morphs into a gray page layout with labeled sections; 28-38s: caption: 'Decide the rooms before the curtains'. |
| 3 | 1:21 | 39 | There are four fidelity levels. Sketches on paper or a whiteboard, boxes and scribbles, perfect for exploring many ideas in minutes. Low-fidelity wireframes, gray boxes with basic labels, for agreeing structure. Mid-fidelity wireframes with real headlines, real form fields and a grid, for testing flows and content. And high-fidelity mockups with final visuals, for approval and handoff. Here's the key idea. Moving to high fidelity too early makes people comment on photos and colors instead of structure. Start low, and only increase fidelity when the question you're answering needs it. | Four fidelity levels<br>• Sketch: explore fast<br>• Low-fi: agree structure<br>• Mid-fi: test flows and content<br>• High-fi: approve and hand off | Layout: four panels progressing left to right. 0-10s: a rough sketch appears; 10-20s: it becomes gray boxes; 20-29s: real headlines and fields fill in; 29-39s: full color and images appear, with a warning icon above the last panel saying 'not yet'. |
| 4 | 2:00 | 45 | Wireframes work best with real content. Lorem ipsum hides problems. A headline that looks fine as placeholder text might be twice as long in reality, or a testimonial might not exist yet. So before you draw a single box, write a content priority list. For an online course page, it might be: the headline with the core promise, a subheadline on who it's for, the primary call to action, proof like testimonials, what you'll learn, the curriculum, the instructor, pricing, the FAQ, and a final call to action. That order reflects what visitors need to decide, not what the company wants to say first. | Content first<br>• Lorem ipsum hides problems<br>• Write a content priority list<br>• Order = what visitors need to decide | Layout: numbered list feeding a wireframe. 0-11s: a lorem ipsum block appears and gets crossed out; 11-22s: a numbered list of ten items types in; 22-34s: each item drops into a stacked section; 34-45s: the stack forms a mobile page outline. |
| 5 | 2:45 | 40 | Now, mobile first. Start with the phone-sized wireframe. A narrow screen has one column, so it forces you to decide what comes first. There's no room for everything above the fold side by side. Once the mobile version works, expand to tablet and desktop, where content can sit next to each other. On mobile, check three things. Is the main message and call to action visible without scrolling far? Are tap targets large and well spaced? And does every section earn its place? If a section doesn't help someone decide, it goes. | Mobile first<br>• One column forces priority<br>• Expand to tablet and desktop later<br>• Check: CTA visible, tap targets, every section earns its place | Layout: phone frame expanding to desktop. 0-10s: a phone wireframe shows a single column; 10-20s: three check icons appear beside it; 20-30s: the frame widens to tablet with two columns; 30-40s: it widens again to desktop with side-by-side content. |
| 6 | 3:25 | 40 | Worked example one, simple. A creator selling a video editing course sketches three mobile layouts in ten minutes. Version A leads with a hero video. Version B leads with student results. Version C leads with the price and a short curriculum. She shows them to two past students and asks which would make them keep reading. Both pick B, because proof of results answers their biggest worry: will this actually work for someone like me? <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Sketching and wireframing

```text
Have you ever sat in a meeting where everyone argued about the color of a button, on a page whose structure was completely wrong? It happens all the time. And it's expensive, because by the time someone notices the structure problem, the page is already designed, built and paid for. Wireframes prevent that. In this lecture, you'll learn what wireframes are, the fidelity levels and when to use each, how to start from a content priority list, why mobile comes first, how to annotate behavior, and how to build a clean wireframe in Figma in about twenty minutes.
```

Scene 2 — What a wireframe is

```text
A wireframe is a simplified layout that shows structure, content priority and function, without final colors, images or polished typography. Why bother? Because wireframes let you agree on what goes where, and why, before anyone spends time on visuals or code. Here's an analogy. Before building a house, an architect draws a floor plan. Nobody chooses curtains from a floor plan. You decide where the kitchen goes, whether the bedrooms get light, and whether the front door leads somewhere sensible. A wireframe is your page's floor plan.
```

Scene 3 — Four fidelity levels

```text
There are four fidelity levels. Sketches on paper or a whiteboard, boxes and scribbles, perfect for exploring many ideas in minutes. Low-fidelity wireframes, gray boxes with basic labels, for agreeing structure. Mid-fidelity wireframes with real headlines, real form fields and a grid, for testing flows and content. And high-fidelity mockups with final visuals, for approval and handoff. Here's the key idea. Moving to high fidelity too early makes people comment on photos and colors instead of structure. Start low, and only increase fidelity when the question you're answering needs it.
```

Scene 4 — Content first

```text
Wireframes work best with real content. Lorem ipsum hides problems. A headline that looks fine as placeholder text might be twice as long in reality, or a testimonial might not exist yet. So before you draw a single box, write a content priority list. For an online course page, it might be: the headline with the core promise, a subheadline on who it's for, the primary call to action, proof like testimonials, what you'll learn, the curriculum, the instructor, pricing, the FAQ, and a final call to action. That order reflects what visitors need to decide, not what the company wants to say first.
```

Scene 5 — Mobile first

```text
Now, mobile first. Start with the phone-sized wireframe. A narrow screen has one column, so it forces you to decide what comes first. There's no room for everything above the fold side by side. Once the mobile version works, expand to tablet and desktop, where content can sit next to each other. On mobile, check three things. Is the main message and call to action visible without scrolling far? Are tap targets large and well spaced? And does every section earn its place? If a section doesn't help someone decide, it goes.
```

Scene 6 — Worked example 1: three sketches in ten minutes

```text
Worked example one, simple. A creator selling a video editing course sketches three mobile layouts in ten minutes. Version A leads with a hero video. Version B leads with student results. Version C leads with the price and a short curriculum. She shows them to two past students and asks which would make them keep reading. Both pick B, because proof of results answers their biggest worry: will this actually work for someone like me? She turns B into a low-fidelity wireframe with real copy and annotations, and only then starts visual design.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 2: Dubai cleaning company (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. A cleaning company in Dubai wants a new homepage. Research shows customers want four things: a price estimate, availability, trust that staff are vetted, and easy booking, often through WhatsApp. So the content priority list starts with a headline and an instant price estimator, then book now and chat on WhatsApp buttons, trust signals like vetting, insurance and reviews, services, a three-step how it works, areas covered, and an FAQ. The owner reviews a low-fidelity version focused only on structure. Then a mid-fidelity version with real copy goes into usability testing.
```

Scene 8 — Watch me do it: Figma wireframe

```text
Watch me do it in Figma. I press F for the frame tool and pick a phone preset. I name it landing, mobile, version one. I add a layout grid: four columns with sixteen pixel margins and gutters. Then I draw one rectangle per item on my content list, select them all, and press shift and A to wrap them in a vertical auto layout frame, with twenty-four pixels between sections. Now reordering is just dragging, and the spacing stays consistent. I paste in real copy, mark images with a box and an X, and label every button.
```

Scene 9 — Annotate and compare

```text
Next, annotations. I add numbered notes. One: headline, maximum ten words. Two: the call to action scrolls to pricing. Three: testimonials become a carousel on mobile and three columns on desktop, real customers only. Four: the form has name and email with inline validation, and a success message replaces the form. Five: a sticky call to action bar appears after half the page is scrolled. Then I duplicate the frame twice to make variants with different section orders, and compare them side by side. In the review, I ask structure questions only, and I park any comments about colors or photos for later.
```

Scene 10 — AI-generated wireframes

```text
A note on AI. Figma's AI features and standalone prompt-to-UI tools can generate a first wireframe from a text description in seconds. That's genuinely useful for exploring layouts. But generated layouts tend to be generic. They don't know your research, your customers' worries or your content priority. So start from your own content list, and treat a generated wireframe as one more variant to compare, never as the answer. We'll go deeper into these tools in the next lessons.
```

Scene 11 — Run a structure-only review

```text
How do you run a wireframe review without it turning into a debate about taste? Use five structure questions. What do you think this page offers, and who is it for? What's the one action we want, and is it visible without much scrolling? Which objection is still unanswered before we ask for that action? Which section could we remove without losing anything? And is anything here only because a competitor has it? Keep a parking lot for comments about colors, fonts and photos, and promise to revisit them at the visual design stage. That keeps the meeting short and the decisions about structure.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Jumping straight into high-fidelity visuals. Using placeholder text that hides content problems. Designing desktop first and cramming everything onto mobile. Leaving wireframes unannotated, so developers and copywriters have to guess the behavior. And letting stakeholders debate photos during a structure review. How do you know wireframing is working? Stakeholders agree on structure before visual design begins, later usability tests find few structural problems, and the number of structural changes requested after high-fidelity design starts goes down over time.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Wireframe structure before style. Start with a content priority list and real copy. Design mobile first. Use the lowest fidelity that answers your question. Annotate behavior. And run reviews that focus on structure. Try this now. Write a content priority list for one landing page you own. Then sketch three different mobile layouts in fifteen minutes. Pick the best, turn it into an annotated low-fidelity wireframe in Figma or on paper, and show it to one person who matches your audience.
```

</details>
