<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — UI fundamentals, components and states

```text
Here's a small mystery. A shop sees lots of duplicate orders. Same customer, same product, two payments, a few seconds apart. Is it fraud? No. It's a button. The pay now button didn't change when tapped, so people tapped it again. One missing loading state, and the shop is processing refunds every week. In this lecture, you'll learn how to apply visual hierarchy on screens, which UI patterns users already understand, how components and design systems help marketing teams move faster, why every element needs states, and how to build a proper button component in Figma.
```

Scene 2 — Why UI matters

```text
Why does UI matter for marketers? Because the interface is where intention turns into action. A clear primary button, a familiar pattern, a visible response when you tap. These reduce effort, and less effort means more completed actions. Think of UI patterns like road signs. A stop sign is red and octagonal in many countries. You don't read it. You recognize it. If a city invented its own shape for stop signs, drivers would hesitate, and accidents would rise. Unusual interface patterns do the same to your visitors. Familiar beats clever almost every time.
```

Scene 3 — Hierarchy on screens

```text
Start with hierarchy on screens. One primary action per view. Make the main button visually dominant, and make secondary actions look secondary, as outlined or text buttons. Make content scannable, with headings, short paragraphs and bullets. Use consistent spacing from a scale, like multiples of four or eight pixels. Keep body text readable, commonly starting around sixteen pixels on the web, with comfortable line height. And respect affordances. Things that look tappable should be tappable, and things that aren't shouldn't look like buttons. When people click on a picture that does nothing, that's a broken affordance.
```

Scene 4 — Familiar UI patterns

```text
Now common patterns people already know. A hero section at the top of landing pages, with a headline, supporting line, call to action and relevant image. Cards for products, articles and case studies, with a consistent layout. Accordions for FAQs, which work well on mobile. Modals for focused tasks, like booking, but easy to close and never shown the moment someone arrives. A sticky call to action bar on mobile, as long as it doesn't cover content. Progress indicators for multi-step forms. And toasts, those small temporary messages, for brief confirmations, but never for critical errors.
```

Scene 5 — Components and design systems

```text
Components and design systems. A component is a reusable interface element, like a button, input, card or navigation bar. A design system is a collection of components, styles for color, type and spacing, patterns and guidelines, used consistently. Why should marketers care? Because landing pages can be assembled quickly from approved sections. Consistency builds trust and recognition. And developers build faster when designs match existing components. Even if you use a website builder, you can work this way. Define your own set of approved blocks: hero, social proof, features, pricing, FAQ and call to action.
```

Scene 6 — States: the forgotten half

```text
Here's the forgotten half of UI: states. Every interactive element has several. A button can be default, hover on desktop, focus for keyboard users, pressed, disabled and loading. An input can be empty, focused, filled, error, success or disabled. A whole page can be loading, empty, error or success. Designing only the default state leads to confusion. A loading state on pay now prevents double payments. A visible focus state is essential for keyboard users and required for accessibility. And disabled buttons should explain why, or better, stay enabled and show clear errors on submit.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: pricing section

```text
Worked example one, simple. A pricing section. Before: three plans with identical styling, eight bullet points each, gray buy buttons, and a monthly or annual toggle that doesn't clearly change the price. After: cards with a consistent structure, and the recommended plan highlighted with a most popular label, only if it's true. Short feature lists that focus on differences, with a full comparison in an expandable table. A specific button on each plan, like start with growth. A toggle that updates prices instantly and shows the annual total. And designed focus and loading states.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: duplicate orders (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. An online electronics store in Lahore notices around one in fifty orders is a duplicate, each needing a manual refund. Session recordings show the pattern: shoppers tap pay now, the button doesn't change for several seconds while the bank verification loads, and they tap again. The fix is a loading state that disables repeat taps, changes the label to processing, and keeps the button the same width so nothing jumps. They also add a toast saying payment received once it's done. Duplicate orders drop to almost none, and so do angry support messages.
```

Scene 9 — Watch me do it: variables + component

```text
Watch me do it in Figma. First, I open the variables panel and create a color collection: brand six hundred, brand seven hundred, text on brand, and border focus. I check each text and background pair for contrast before I save it. Then spacing variables: eight, twelve and sixteen. Next, I type a label, press shift and A for auto layout, set the padding using my spacing variables, and apply the colors. I press control or command, alt, and K to make it a component. Now any copy I place will update when I change the main one.
```

Scene 10 — States as variants

```text
Now the states. I add a variant property called state, with default, hover, focus, pressed, disabled and loading. The focus variant gets a two pixel outline in my focus color, offset from the button so it's clearly visible. The loading variant gets a spinner and the label processing. Then I add a type property with primary and secondary, so the secondary button is visibly quieter. Finally, I check size. At least twenty-four by twenty-four CSS pixels is the WCAG two point two minimum, and I aim for around forty-four to forty-eight pixels tall for comfortable thumbs. I paste the spec into the component description.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Several equally prominent buttons competing for attention. Designing only the default state. Tiny tap targets packed tightly together. Inventing unusual patterns where familiar ones work better. Styling one-off buttons by hand instead of using a component or saved style. And a focus outline removed because someone thought it looked ugly. That last one locks out keyboard users. How do you measure success? Fewer inconsistencies in design review, faster page assembly from brief to live, and fewer double submissions and rage clicks in your recordings.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Keep one primary action per view, and make secondary actions look secondary. Use familiar patterns people already understand. Build with reusable components or approved blocks. Design every state, especially loading, focus, error and empty. And give touch targets enough size and spacing. Try this now. Pick one section of your site, like pricing, sign-up or a product card. List every state its components need, then build or sketch the missing loading, error and focus states. If you use Figma, turn your main button into a component with state variants.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Figma and AI design tools

```text
You can now type a sentence and get a working landing page prototype in under a minute. That's real, and it's changing how marketing teams work. But here's the catch. The same tools will happily invent testimonials, skip focus states, use gray text nobody can read, and produce a page that looks exactly like everyone else's. In this lecture, you'll learn the core Figma concepts every marketer should know, what today's AI design tools actually do, a workflow that keeps humans in charge, and how to audit AI output before it reaches a customer.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter for marketers? Speed. You can explore five layout ideas before lunch, test an interactive calculator with real users before a developer writes a line of code, and brief developers with precise designs instead of screenshots. But speed without judgment just produces bad pages faster. Think of AI design tools like a very fast junior designer who has seen millions of websites but never met your customers. Brilliant at drafts. Terrible at knowing what your audience worries about, what's legally true, or what your brand stands for. You're the art director.
```

Scene 3 — Figma essentials

```text
First, the Figma basics. Frames are containers for screens or sections, like artboards. Auto layout makes frames stack and space their contents automatically, so sections reflow when copy changes. Components and variants are reusable elements with properties like type and state. Change the main one and every copy updates. Styles and variables store named colors, type and spacing, including modes like light and dark. Prototyping links frames into clickable flows. Dev Mode gives developers measurements and code hints. And FigJam is the whiteboard for research, flows and workshops. You've already used several of these in earlier lessons.
```

Scene 4 — AI inside Figma

```text
Now the AI layer inside Figma. Figma's AI tools can generate first-draft layouts from a prompt, rename and organize layers, rewrite or translate text, generate or edit images, and wire up quick prototype connections. The feature called First Draft generates editable wireframes and designs from a description. And from May twenty twenty-six, Figma introduced an agent, in beta and rolling out gradually, as the new entry point for that work, with re-prompting and bulk edits. These features use AI credits, which vary by plan and seat. Features and limits change often, so always check Figma's current documentation.
```

Scene 5 — Prompt-to-prototype tools

```text
Next, prompt to prototype. Figma Make turns a prompt, and optionally an existing design, into a working interactive prototype or web app built from code. You can try it on the free Starter plan, with more capability on paid plans. Outside Figma, Google's Stitch generates multi-screen interface designs and exports to Figma, and code-first tools like Vercel's v0 and Lovable generate working front ends from prompts. Website builders such as Framer and Webflow have AI generation too. Here's the key idea. These tools are superb for exploration and throwaway prototypes. They are not a substitute for research, accessibility checks or brand judgment.
```

Scene 6 — Figma MCP server

```text
For teams with developers, there's one more piece: the Figma MCP server. MCP, the Model Context Protocol, is an open standard that lets AI assistants connect to tools and data. Figma's MCP server lets AI coding tools like Claude Code, Cursor or Copilot in VS Code read real design context. The layer structure, the auto layout rules, the variables and the components. Not just a screenshot. And with Code Connect, design components map to the matching components in your codebase. So when an assistant builds a page, it reuses your real button instead of inventing a new one. That's how design systems survive the AI era.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: creator waitlist (illustrative)

```text
Worked example one, simple. A creator in London wants to test a new fitness program. She writes a content priority list from her DMs, then prompts Figma Make for a mobile waitlist page with a short quiz. She generates three versions. She keeps the quiz from one and the structure from another, and rebuilds the page with her saved brand colors. Her audit catches two problems: an invented ten thousand members badge, and low-contrast gray body text. Both get fixed. Five followers try the prototype over video. Three misunderstand one quiz question, so she rewrites it before building the real page.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: agency audit page (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. A marketing agency in Riyadh offers free ad account audits. The strategist writes a detailed prompt: audience, goal, content order, the headline, three benefits, a how it works section, placeholder testimonials clearly marked, an FAQ, and constraints. Sixteen pixel body text, forty-four pixel buttons, brand teal buttons with white text, visible focus styles, no fake urgency, no invented numbers. The first output ignores the content order and uses a default template. She re-prompts, then audits. The teal and white pairing needs checking against four point five to one. The developer then builds it through the MCP server using real components.
```

Scene 9 — Watch me do it: prompt, generate, audit

```text
Watch me do it. I start with my content priority list, not the tool. In the prompt, I describe the audience, the one primary action, the content in order, and hard constraints: minimum text size, button height, brand color, visible focus, no fake urgency, no invented statistics. I generate three options and treat each as a sketch. Then I run the audit checklist. Does the structure follow my list? Any invented claims, numbers or logos? Text contrast at least four point five to one? Targets big enough, focus visible, fields labeled? Does it work at three hundred twenty pixels wide? Finally, I rebuild the winner with our components.
```

Scene 10 — Rules for AI design

```text
A few rules keep you safe. Privacy: don't paste customer data or unreleased plans into tools your organization hasn't approved, and check each tool's data-use settings. Intellectual property: don't prompt a tool to copy a competitor's site or a living designer's distinctive style. Use your own brand assets and licensed images. Truthfulness: AI tools invent testimonials, statistics and client logos. Remove them. Fake proof is a consumer protection problem, not just a design flaw. And accessibility: generated designs often miss focus states, labels and contrast. Audit every single time.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Prompting with a vibe, like make it modern, instead of your content and constraints. Accepting the tool's default template structure. Shipping AI output straight to customers without an audit. Letting generated designs drift from your components and brand variables, so every page looks slightly different. And confusing speed with success. How do you measure success? Time from idea to testable prototype, the number of options explored, and above all, usability test results and live metrics. AI speed only counts if the page performs at least as well as your hand-built baseline and passes the accessibility audit.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Know the Figma essentials: frames, auto layout, components, variables, prototyping and Dev Mode. Use AI tools like Figma's AI, Figma Make, Stitch, v0 and Lovable for fast exploration, and treat their output as sketches. Prompt with your research and constraints. Audit every output for invented claims, accessibility and structure. And if you have developers, use the MCP server and Code Connect so code reuses your real components. Try this now. Write a content-rich prompt for one landing page, generate three options, run the audit checklist on the best one, and rebuild it with your own components or saved styles.
```

</details>

| 3 | 1:21 | 40 | Match fidelity to your question. A paper prototype, sketches you operate by swapping sheets, is perfect for very early concept tests. And a coded prototype or unpublished staging page tests performance and real data. So, can users find pricing? Clickable wireframes will do. Does the new visual style feel premium? You need high fidelity. Don't spend a week polishing visuals to answer a structural question. | Match fidelity to the question<br>• Paper: early concepts<br>• Clickable wireframes: flows<br>• High-fi: details and approval<br>• Coded/staging: performance, real data | Layout: four-level ladder with questions attached. 0-10s: a paper sketch sits on the bottom rung; 10-20s: clickable wireframes and high-fidelity screens climb the ladder; 20-30s: a staging page sits at the top; 30-40s: question tags attach to the right rungs. |
| 4 | 2:01 | 34 | What should you prototype? The parts where uncertainty or risk is highest. New flows, like booking, checkout or onboarding. What don't you need to prototype? Small copy tweaks on low-traffic pages, or well-understood patterns you've tested before. Here's the key idea. Prototype in proportion to risk. | What to prototype<br>• New flows<br>• High-traffic, high-spend pages<br>• Complex interactions<br>• Key messaging order<br>• Prototype in proportion to risk | Layout: risk matrix with items placed. 0-8s: a two-by-two matrix of uncertainty and impact appears; 8-17s: checkout, calculator and onboarding cards land in the high-risk corner; 17-26s: a small copy tweak lands in the low corner; 26-34s: the high corner glows with 'Prototype this'. |
| 5 | 2:35 | 43 | Worked example one, simple. A small online shop wants to add a gift message option at checkout. They build two clickable prototypes. Version A: a checkbox that reveals a text field. In handoff, the designer includes every state: empty, typing, character limit reached, and error. Plus helper copy, and a tracking event called gift message added. QA confirms the message actually prints on the packing slip. | Worked example 1: gift message option<br>• A: checkbox reveals field<br>• B: separate step<br>• Five tests → checkbox wins<br>• States, copy, tracking, packing-slip QA | Layout: two prototype flows side by side. 0-11s: version A shows a checkbox revealing a text field; 11-22s: version B shows an extra step screen; 22-32s: five user icons vote and A gets the tick; 32-43s: a packing slip prints with the gift message. |

| 8 | 4:45 | 47 | Now handoff, which is where good designs often get lost. Developers need more than pretty screens. They need final designs for each breakpoint. And acceptance criteria, meaning what done looks like. Dev Mode and the MCP server help, but a short handoff note at the top of the file still saves hours. | Complete handoff<br>• Breakpoints and every state<br>• Tokens and named components<br>• Copy, alt text, accessibility notes<br>• Tracking plan + acceptance criteria | Layout: handoff note document building up. 0-12s: a markdown-style handoff note types in at the top of a design file; 12-24s: sections for states, tokens and copy tick off; 24-35s: accessibility and tracking lines highlight; 35-47s: an acceptance criteria box closes the note. |
| 9 | 5:32 | 46 | Many marketers build pages themselves in no-code builders. The same discipline applies. Build from approved sections. Check every breakpoint, because builders often produce odd mobile layouts unless you adjust them. And test the form end to end. Does the submission actually arrive? Is the confirmation right? Does your CRM receive the lead? Then run pre-launch QA. At least one iPhone and one Android phone. Every link and button. Forms submit, validate, confirm and store data. Tracking fires and respects consent. Acceptable load on mobile data. Keyboard navigation with visible focus. Prices, dates and legal text checked. Right to left layouts correct, if you have them. | No-code builders and pre-launch QA<br>• Approved sections; check breakpoints<br>• Test forms end to end, into the CRM<br>• Two phones, links, tracking, speed<br>• Keyboard, legal text, RTL | Layout: QA checklist beside two phones. 0-12s: an iPhone and an Android phone appear; 12-23s: a form submits and a CRM icon receives a lead; 23-34s: checklist items tick one by one; 34-46s: a final green 'Ready to launch' stamp appears. |
| 10 | 6:18 | 50 | How do you actually test a prototype? Keep it simple. Recruit three to five people who match your audience. Give each person a realistic scenario, like you want to join next week's webinar, see if you can sign up, rather than click the green button. Ask them to think aloud. Stay quiet, and don't rescue them when they get stuck, because that's exactly the moment you need to see. Note where they hesitate, what they expected to happen, and what they said. Prototypes do have limits. People know nothing is real, so they may skip reading or accept slow steps. Use prototypes for flow and comprehension, and confirm performance and real data on a staging page. | Testing a prototype<br>• 3 to 5 people from your audience<br>• Realistic scenarios, think aloud<br>• Don't rescue; note hesitations<br>• Limits: confirm speed and data on staging | Layout: video call test with a prototype on screen. 0-12s: a participant tile and a shared prototype appear; 12-25s: a scenario card slides in; 25-38s: hesitation markers drop on the timeline as the participant pauses; 38-50s: a note card reads 'Confirm speed on staging'. |

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Prototyping and handoff

```text
Here's a painful story. A shop spends two weeks building a new checkout step. It launches on a Friday. By Monday, customer service is flooded with confused messages, and the team spends another two weeks fixing it. The frustrating part? Five people clicking through a prototype for twenty minutes each would have caught the problem before anyone wrote code. In this lecture, you'll learn what prototypes are and how to match fidelity to your question, what to prototype first, how to build a clickable prototype in Figma, how to hand off designs developers can build correctly, and how to QA before launch.
```

Scene 2 — What a prototype is

```text
A prototype is an interactive simulation of an experience. Clickable screens that let people try a flow before it's built. Why does it matter? Because prototypes are cheap to change, and live websites are not. Think of a dress rehearsal in theater. The actors walk through the whole show with props and costumes before opening night. Mistakes in rehearsal are funny. Mistakes on opening night are expensive. A prototype is your dress rehearsal. You find the confusing moments while fixing them still takes minutes, not sprints.
```

Scene 3 — Match fidelity to the question

```text
Match fidelity to your question. A paper prototype, sketches you operate by swapping sheets, is perfect for very early concept tests. Clickable wireframes, with low or mid-fidelity screens linked together, test flows and structure. A high-fidelity prototype with near-final visuals tests details, gets approval and guides developers. And a coded prototype or unpublished staging page tests performance and real data. So, can users find pricing? Clickable wireframes will do. Does the new visual style feel premium? You need high fidelity. Don't spend a week polishing visuals to answer a structural question.
```

Scene 4 — What to prototype

```text
What should you prototype? The parts where uncertainty or risk is highest. New flows, like booking, checkout or onboarding. Pages with high traffic or high ad spend, where a small problem costs a lot. Complex interactions, like filters, calculators or pricing toggles. And the content order and messaging on key pages. What don't you need to prototype? Small copy tweaks on low-traffic pages, or well-understood patterns you've tested before. Here's the key idea. Prototype in proportion to risk.
```

Scene 5 — Worked example 1: gift message option

```text
Worked example one, simple. A small online shop wants to add a gift message option at checkout. They build two clickable prototypes. Version A: a checkbox that reveals a text field. Version B: a separate gift step. Five customers try both over video calls. The checkbox version is faster and causes less confusion, because the separate step makes people think they're being upsold. In handoff, the designer includes every state: empty, typing, character limit reached, and error. Plus helper copy, and a tracking event called gift message added. QA confirms the message actually prints on the packing slip.
```

Scene 6 — Worked example 2: onboarding (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. An education startup in Islamabad plans a new onboarding flow for parents, expected to take a developer three weeks. Instead, the designer builds a high-fidelity prototype in two days and tests it with six parents. Four of them stall on the same screen, which asks them to choose between two plan names that mean nothing to them. The fix, renaming the plans by outcome and adding a one-line comparison, takes an hour in the prototype. The developer builds the corrected version once. No launch-week firefighting.
```

Scene 7 — Watch me do it: clickable prototype

```text
Watch me do it in Figma. I create one frame for each screen or state: landing, form, form with error, and confirmation. I switch to the prototype tab. I select the call to action button, drag the blue connector to the form frame, and set on tap, navigate to. I connect the submit button to the confirmation frame. For the menu, I use open overlay and turn on close when clicking outside. I set a flow starting point on the landing frame and name the flow register, mobile. Then I press present, test it on my own phone, and share the prototype link with view access.
```

Scene 8 — Complete handoff

```text
Now handoff, which is where good designs often get lost. Developers need more than pretty screens. They need final designs for each breakpoint. Every state, including hover, focus, error, loading, empty and success. Spacing, type and colors as tokens or variables. Components named like the ones in the codebase. Exported assets. Interaction notes for animations, sticky elements and modals. Final copy, alt text and error messages. Accessibility notes on heading order, labels and focus order. A tracking plan. And acceptance criteria, meaning what done looks like. Dev Mode and the MCP server help, but a short handoff note at the top of the file still saves hours.
```

Scene 9 — No-code builders and pre-launch QA

```text
Many marketers build pages themselves in no-code builders. The same discipline applies. Build from approved sections. Check every breakpoint, because builders often produce odd mobile layouts unless you adjust them. And test the form end to end. Does the submission actually arrive? Is the confirmation right? Does your CRM receive the lead? Then run pre-launch QA. At least one iPhone and one Android phone. Every link and button. Forms submit, validate, confirm and store data. Tracking fires and respects consent. Acceptable load on mobile data. Keyboard navigation with visible focus. Prices, dates and legal text checked. Right to left layouts correct, if you have them.
```

Scene 10 — Testing a prototype

```text
How do you actually test a prototype? Keep it simple. Recruit three to five people who match your audience. Give each person a realistic scenario, like you want to join next week's webinar, see if you can sign up, rather than click the green button. Ask them to think aloud. Stay quiet, and don't rescue them when they get stuck, because that's exactly the moment you need to see. Note where they hesitate, what they expected to happen, and what they said. Prototypes do have limits. People know nothing is real, so they may skip reading or accept slow steps. Use prototypes for flow and comprehension, and confirm performance and real data on a staging page.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Skipping prototypes for high-risk flows. Prototypes that only show the happy path, with no error or empty states. Handing over designs without states, copy or accessibility notes. Launching without testing forms and tracking end to end. And assuming that because a form says thanks, the lead reached the CRM. How do you measure success? Compare issues found in prototype tests with issues found after launch. A good prototyping habit shifts problems earlier, when they're cheap. For handoff, track design versus build mismatches found in QA, and the time from ready for dev to launch.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Prototype at the fidelity your question needs, and focus on the riskiest parts. Build clickable prototypes with one frame per screen and state, including errors. Hand off with states, tokens, copy, accessibility notes, tracking and acceptance criteria. And QA forms, tracking, devices and accessibility end to end before launch. Try this now. Build a clickable prototype of one flow, like sign-up to confirmation, including at least one error state. Test it with two or three people. Then write a handoff note for it using the template in the lesson.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Anatomy of an effective landing page

```text
Imagine clicking an ad that says free seven-day meal plan for busy parents. You land on a page that says welcome to Healthy Living, with a menu of twelve items, a blog, a shop and a newsletter pop-up. Where's the meal plan? You hesitate for three seconds and leave. That's not a traffic problem. It's a landing page problem. In this lecture, you'll learn the one page, one job principle, how message match works, the core sections of a high-converting page, how to write headlines and calls to action that are specific and honest, and how long a page really needs to be.
```

Scene 2 — One page, one job

```text
A landing page is where visitors arrive from an ad, email, post or search result. The most effective ones have one primary goal. Register. Book. Buy. Download. Every element supports that goal. Why does this matter? Because every extra choice splits attention. Think of a landing page like a shop window with one outfit on display. The window tells you exactly what's new and where the door is. A shop window stuffed with every item in the store tells you nothing. Your paid traffic deserves a focused window, not the whole warehouse.
```

Scene 3 — Message match

```text
Message match means the landing page continues the conversation the ad started. If the ad says free seven-day meal plan for busy parents, the headline should echo that promise. The visuals should feel related. And the offer must be exactly the same, including price, conditions and deadline. The same language too. An Arabic ad should land on an Arabic page. Poor message match makes people wonder if they're in the right place, and a moment of doubt is often enough to lose them. And bait and switch, where the page offers something different from the ad, isn't just bad UX. It can be misleading advertising.
```

Scene 4 — Core sections

```text
Now the core sections. The hero gives instant clarity: a benefit headline, a subheadline on who it's for or how it works, the primary call to action, and a relevant visual. Social proof builds trust with real testimonials, reviews, and client logos used with permission. Benefits explain why it matters, outcomes over features, three to six points. How it works reduces uncertainty in three or four steps. Details answer what exactly do I get. Pricing gives decision information: clear totals, currency, payment options and real guarantees. An FAQ removes blockers using real customer questions. And a final call to action makes the next step easy.
```

Scene 5 — Headlines that work

```text
Headlines. A strong headline is specific and centered on the visitor's outcome. Weak: welcome to BrightPath Academy. Better: learn to edit short videos for clients in four weeks. Stronger, with the audience named: freelancers, learn to edit short-form videos clients pay for, in four weeks. How do you check clarity? The five-second test. Show the page for five seconds, hide it, and ask what it offers and who it's for. If people can't answer, your headline isn't doing its job. No amount of design polish will fix a vague promise.
```

Scene 6 — CTAs and trust

```text
Calls to action. Use one primary action, repeated at logical points: after the hero, after the proof, and at the end. Make the label specific. Book my free call beats submit. Reduce risk near the button with honest reassurance: free, cancel anytime, no card needed. But only if it's true. On mobile, a sticky call to action bar can help, as long as it doesn't cover content or the cookie banner. And trust. Show business details, privacy and refund terms. Use genuine security indicators on payment steps. Never use as seen in logos without real coverage, or numbers you can't back up.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: free checklist

```text
Worked example one, simple. A free checklist download for small business owners. Because the commitment is low, the page is short. A headline that repeats the ad's promise: the twenty-point checklist to find wasted ad spend. Three benefits. A preview image of the checklist. A two-field form. And a button: send me the checklist. No pricing, no long FAQ, no company history. Short offers need short pages. The team checks message match against the ad, runs a five-second test with two people, and launches.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Riyadh agency audit (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. A marketing agency in Riyadh offers a free thirty-minute ad account audit, advertised in Arabic and English. Each ad lands on a page in the matching language. The hero says free thirty-minute ad account audit, find wasted spend before your next campaign, with a button: book my free audit. Proof: three client testimonials used with permission, and certifications shown only where genuinely held. What you get: a recorded audit, three priority fixes, no obligation. How it works: book, share read-only access, get your audit call. The FAQ answers do you need admin access, and is my data safe.
```

Scene 9 — Watch me do it: the landing page brief

```text
Watch me do it. Before I touch a page builder, I fill in a one-page brief. I copy the ad's exact promise. I write the audience and their main worry. One primary goal as a single verb, and the call to action label. The offer details, which must match the ad. The proof we can truthfully show. The top five objections from DMs and sales calls. The trust and legal information we need. And the primary metric with its baseline, plus a guardrail. Then I map each section to a brief field. Any section with no matching field gets cut.
```

Scene 10 — How long should it be?

```text
How long should the page be? There's no single right length. The more a visitor has to commit, in money, time or personal data, the more information they usually need. A free checklist can succeed with a headline, three benefits and a form. A high-priced course or consulting service often needs proof, detail and answers to objections. Use your research. If people keep asking the same questions before buying, the answers belong on the page. You can also use AI to draft ten headline options from the ad promise. Then test the best two or three with real people.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Multiple competing goals on one page: subscribe, follow, buy and read the blog. Generic headlines about the company instead of the visitor's outcome. Fake scarcity or fake testimonials. Hiding the price or key conditions until late. And pages that don't match the language or offer of the ad. How do you measure success? Conversion rate from the campaign's traffic to the single goal, split by device and source. Plus guardrails: refund or no-show rate, lead quality and complaints. A page that converts more but brings worse customers hasn't improved.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Give each landing page one job. Match the message that brought people there: promise, offer, visuals and language. Structure sections around what visitors need to decide. Write specific, outcome-focused headlines and calls to action. Build trust with honest, evidenced proof and clear terms. And scale the page's length with the commitment you're asking for. Try this now. Pick one ad or post and its landing page. Run the message-match checklist, do a five-second test with two people, then rewrite the headline and the primary call to action.
```

</details>

### 12. Mobile-first design

| DevTools | dev tools |
| PDF | P D F |

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0:00 | 44 | Here's a quick experiment. Look at your latest campaign report and find the split between mobile and desktop visitors. For traffic from social media and messaging apps, mobile is often the clear majority. Now, where was the landing page designed and approved? Probably on a large monitor in an office. That gap between where pages are made and where they're used is one of the most expensive blind spots in marketing. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Mobile-first design

```text
Here's a quick experiment. Look at your latest campaign report and find the split between mobile and desktop visitors. For traffic from social media and messaging apps, mobile is often the clear majority. Now, where was the landing page designed and approved? Probably on a large monitor in an office. That gap between where pages are made and where they're used is one of the most expensive blind spots in marketing. In this lecture, you'll learn why mobile-first works, the core mobile design principles, how to handle forms and in-app browsers, and a practical QA routine with DevTools and real phones.
```

Scene 2 — Why mobile-first works

```text
Mobile-first design means designing for small screens, touch input and variable connections first, then enhancing for larger screens. Why does it work? It forces prioritization, because one column means you must decide what matters most. It improves performance, because you use fewer, lighter elements. It aligns with how Google evaluates sites, since Google uses mobile-first indexing, primarily using the mobile version of content for indexing and ranking. And it reflects real usage. In many markets, including Pakistan, the Gulf and much of the world, the phone is the main or only way people get online.
```

Scene 3 — The packing analogy

```text
Here's an analogy. Packing for a weekend trip with one small bag. You can't take everything, so you choose what really matters. Then, if you're given a bigger suitcase, adding things is easy. Doing it the other way around is painful. Pack a huge suitcase first, then try to squeeze it into a small bag, and you end up sitting on it, with socks hanging out. That's desktop-first design. Mobile-first is packing the small bag first. The essentials are guaranteed. Everything else is a bonus for bigger screens.
```

Scene 4 — Mobile design principles

```text
Now the principles. Thumb-friendly: key actions within reach, with large targets, at least WCAG's minimum and ideally around forty-four to forty-eight pixels, and spacing between links. Readable: body text around sixteen pixels or larger, short paragraphs, good contrast. Scannable: clear headings, bullets and accordions for detail. Fast: compressed images, few scripts, no heavy background video. Focused: one primary call to action and simplified navigation. Forgiving: easy to correct mistakes, with autofill and the right keyboards. And context-aware: tap to call, WhatsApp links, maps and wallet payments where relevant.
```

Scene 5 — Responsive transformations

```text
Responsive layouts adapt to screen width. On mobile: one column, stacked sections, full-width buttons. On tablet: two columns where helpful. On desktop: multi-column layouts, with images and text side by side. The trick is deciding how each section transforms. A row of three cards becomes a stack, or a horizontal swipe. A comparison table becomes stacked cards, or a scrollable table with a sticky first column. Plan these transformations in your wireframes, instead of letting a page builder decide for you.
```

Scene 6 — Mobile pitfalls

```text
Watch out for mobile pitfalls. Intrusive interstitials: full-screen pop-ups on arrival frustrate people, and Google has guidance against intrusive interstitials on mobile. Sticky element pile-ups: a header, a chat bubble, a cookie banner and a call to action bar all stacked until there's barely any content visible. Hover-only interactions that don't work on touch. Tiny text inside images, like infographics nobody can read on a phone. And horizontal scrolling caused by one oversized element. Then there are in-app browsers. Links from social apps often open inside the app, where autofill, logins and some payment flows may behave differently.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: restaurant ordering

```text
Worked example one, simple. A restaurant ordering page. Before: a menu designed for desktop, with small text, a PDF menu link, prices that only appear on hover, and a phone number that isn't tappable. After: a mobile-first menu with categories as sticky tabs. Dishes as cards with photos, prices and add buttons. Tap to call and WhatsApp buttons. An Arabic and English language switch. And a checkout with wallet payments and cash on delivery where relevant. Every fix removes effort for a hungry person holding a phone in one hand.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: skincare brand (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. A skincare brand in Jeddah sees mobile conversion at roughly a third of desktop from the same Instagram campaign. A QA session inside Instagram's in-app browser reveals three problems. The phone field is a plain text field, so people get a letter keyboard and make mistakes. A chat bubble covers the checkout button on smaller phones. And a full-screen discount pop-up appears before the product loads. The team fixes the input type, moves the chat bubble, and delays the pop-up until after the visitor has engaged. The mobile gap narrows over the following weeks.
```

Scene 9 — Watch me do it: DevTools + real phone

```text
Watch me do it. I open the page in Chrome, press F twelve, and switch on the device toolbar. I test at three hundred twenty, three hundred sixty, three hundred ninety and four hundred thirty pixels wide, looking for horizontal scrolling and overlapping sticky elements. In the network panel, I throttle to slow 4G and reload, noting when the headline and button appear. Then I run a mobile Lighthouse report for performance and accessibility. Lab tests are simulations, so I also check field data from real users in PageSpeed Insights. Finally, I pick up a mid-range Android phone on mobile data, and open the page from inside Instagram.
```

Scene 10 — Fixing what the phone reveals

```text
On that phone I complete the main task, end to end. The phone field shows a letter keyboard, so I check the code. The fix is simple: use type tel and an autocomplete hint of tel, and do the same for email, name and postal code, so the right keyboard appears and the browser can autofill. I check that tap to call uses a tel link, and that the WhatsApp button works inside the in-app browser. Every issue goes in a QA log with the device, the app, the width, a screenshot, severity, the fix, and whether it's been retested.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Designing on desktop and shrinking to mobile. PDF menus or brochures as the main content. Stacking multiple sticky elements. Testing only on flagship phones and fast office Wi-Fi. Never testing inside social in-app browsers. And ignoring performance budgets, so every new tracking pixel or chat widget quietly slows the page. How do you measure success? Compare mobile and desktop conversion for the same traffic source. A large, persistent gap is a signal. Also track mobile Core Web Vitals field data, mobile form completion, and conversions from in-app browser traffic.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Design for small screens, touch and variable connections first. Prioritize one column, make targets thumb-friendly, keep text readable, and make forms easy with the right keyboards and autofill. Avoid intrusive pop-ups and sticky clutter. And test on real mid-range phones, on mobile data, inside the social apps where your ads open. Try this now. Open your key landing page on a mid-range phone using mobile data, from inside one social app. Run the mobile QA checklist, log what you find, and fix the top three issues this week.
```

</details>

| 1 | 0:00 | 44 | Visitors almost never complain about a slow page, a hidden fee or a manipulative countdown timer. They just leave. Or they buy once, feel tricked, and never come back. Or they ask their bank for a chargeback. These are three silent conversion killers: speed, trust and manipulation. In this lecture, you'll learn Google's Core Web Vitals and their thresholds, how to fix the most common performance problems on marketing pages, the trust signals that make people willing to act, how to tell ethical persuasion from dark patterns, and why regulators in the US, UK and EU care about this more than ever. | Speed, trust and ethical persuasion<br>• Three silent conversion killers<br>• Core Web Vitals<br>• Trust signals<br>• Persuasion versus dark patterns | Layout: three silent icons around a checkout. 0-11s: a slow loading spinner appears; 11-22s: a hidden fee line appears on a receipt; 22-33s: a countdown timer resets itself; 33-44s: the three icons fade and the title card appears. |
| 2 | 0:44 | 42 | Why does this matter? Because each of these problems taxes every visitor you pay for. A slow page loses people before they've read a word. Missing trust signals make ready buyers hesitate. And dark patterns might lift a short-term number, but they increase refunds, chargebacks and complaints, and damage the brand. Here's an analogy. Think of a market stall. If the queue is slow, people walk away. If the prices aren't marked, people are suspicious. And if the seller slips an extra item into your bag without asking, you'll never come back, and you'll tell your friends. | Why it matters<br>• Slow pages lose people early<br>• No trust signals, no action<br>• Dark patterns cost refunds and reputation<br>• The market stall analogy | Layout: market stall illustration. 0-10s: a long slow queue forms at a stall; 10-21s: a price-less product appears with a question mark; 21-32s: a hand slips an extra item into a bag; 32-42s: a customer icon walks away and speech bubbles spread to friends. |
| 3 | 1:26 | 44 | Let's start with speed. Google's Core Web Vitals measure three aspects of experience. Largest Contentful Paint, or LCP, measures how quickly the main content appears. Good is two and a half seconds or less. Interaction to Next Paint, or INP, measures how quickly the page responds when someone taps or types. Good is two hundred milliseconds or less. INP replaced an older metric, First Input Delay, in March twenty twenty-four. And Cumulative Layout Shift, or CLS, measures visual stability. Good is zero point one or less. Google judges these at the seventy-fifth percentile of real visits, for mobile and desktop separately. | Core Web Vitals<br>• LCP: main content ≤ 2.5 s<br>• INP: responsiveness ≤ 200 ms<br>• CLS: stability ≤ 0.1<br>• Judged at the 75th percentile | Layout: three gauges with green, amber and red zones. 0-11s: an LCP gauge settles in the green below 2.5 seconds; 11-22s: an INP gauge settles below 200 milliseconds; 22-33s: a CLS gauge settles below 0.1; 33-44s: a label reads '75th percentile of real visits'. |
| 4 | 2:10 | 40 | What typically hurts these numbers on marketing pages? For LCP, huge hero images, slow servers and render-blocking scripts. For INP, heavy JavaScript and too many third-party scripts, like pixels, chat widgets and heatmaps. For CLS, images without dimensions, and banners or ads that load late and push content down, which is how people end up tapping the wrong button. Here's the key idea. Marketers often cause these problems, one tag at a time. So marketers can often fix them too, without deep engineering: compress images, reserve space, and question every script. | What slows marketing pages<br>• LCP: big hero images, slow servers<br>• INP: heavy scripts, too many tags<br>• CLS: missing dimensions, late banners<br>• Marketers add tags; marketers can remove them | Layout: page with problem sources labeled. 0-10s: a giant hero image file size label appears; 10-20s: a stack of third-party tag icons piles up; 20-30s: a late banner pushes content down and a finger mis-taps; 30-40s: the tags are trimmed and the page settles. |
| 5 | 2:50 | 38 | Now trust. Trust decides whether visitors are willing to act at all. The essentials: clear business identity, meaning name, location or contact details, and registration details where relevant. Real testimonials and reviews, used with consent, and not cherry-picked to mislead. Transparent pricing that includes taxes, fees and delivery. Clear refund and cancellation terms. Privacy and cookie information. Reputable payment options, including locally trusted methods. Consistent, professional, error-free design. And a responsive support channel, like chat, email or WhatsApp. None of these are exciting. All of them matter. | Trust signals<br>• Clear business identity<br>• Real reviews, used with consent<br>• All-in pricing; clear refunds<br>• Privacy, trusted payments, support | Layout: checklist building beside a checkout page. 0-10s: business identity and contact details appear in a footer; 10-19s: review cards with consent badges appear; 19-28s: a price breakdown shows taxes and delivery upfront; 28-38s: payment and support icons appear. |
| 6 | 3:28 | 43 | Persuasion helps people make decisions that are good for them, by making benefits, proof and next steps clear. Dark patterns, also called deceptive design, trick or pressure people into things they wouldn't otherwise do. Fake urgency, like a countdown that resets when you refresh. Fake scarcity, like only two left when that isn't true. Confirmshaming, like no thanks, I don't like saving money. Sneaking items into the basket. Hidden costs that appear at the last step. Easy sign-up with very hard cancellation. Free trials that silently convert with no reminder. Pre-ticked consent. And ads disguised as content or reviews. | Persuasion vs dark patterns<br>• Fake urgency or scarcity<br>• Confirmshaming • sneak into basket<br>• Hidden costs • hard cancellation<br>• Silent trial conversion • pre-ticked consent | Layout: two columns labeled Persuasion and Manipulation. 0-11s: ethical examples fill the left column; 11-22s: dark patterns fill the right column one by one; 22-32s: a resetting timer spins in the right column; 32-43s: the right column gets crossed out. |
| 7 | 4:11 | 46 | Regulators care. Here's the picture as of twenty twenty-six, as general information, not legal advice. In the US, the FTC's click to cancel rule was struck down by an appeals court in July twenty twenty-five, and the FTC reopened rulemaking in twenty twenty-six. But the FTC still acts against subscription traps and deceptive design under existing law, and several states have their own auto-renewal laws. In the UK, the Digital Markets, Competition and Consumers Act gave the regulator direct fining powers from April twenty twenty-five, and bans fake reviews and drip pricing. In the EU, the Digital Services Act prohibits deceptive interface design on online platforms. | The 2025–2026 regulatory picture<br>• US: click-to-cancel vacated; rulemaking reopened<br>• FTC still acts on subscription traps<br>• UK DMCC Act: fines, drip pricing banned<br>• EU DSA: no deceptive interfaces | Layout: three regional panels. 0-12s: a US panel shows a court gavel and a 'rulemaking reopened' note; 12-23s: a UK panel shows a fine icon and a hidden-fee receipt crossed out; 23-34s: an EU panel shows a deceptive interface crossed out; 34-46s: caption: 'General information, not legal advice'. |
| 8 | 4:57 | 40 | Worked example one, simple. A subscription sign-up page. Before: the price is shown as from nine dollars, with a twenty-nine dollar setup fee revealed only at checkout. | 11 | 7:06 | 40 | Common mistakes. Adding scripts and widgets without checking performance. Lazy-loading the hero image. Hiding fees to make prices look lower. How do you measure it properly? Track Core Web Vitals field data for your key templates, conversion rate, and quality guardrails: refunds, chargebacks, early cancellations and complaints. Ethical persuasion shows up as steady or rising conversion with falling complaints. | Common mistakes and measures<br>• Unchecked scripts • lazy-loaded hero<br>• Hidden fees • fake timers<br>• Pushy cookie banners<br>• Measure: field vitals + quality guardrails | Layout: mistake cards and a guardrail panel. 0-10s: six mistake cards appear; 10-20s: each flips green; 20-30s: a field-data vitals panel turns green; 30-40s: complaint and refund gauges drop. |
| 12 | 7:46 | 41 | Let's recap. Use Core Web Vitals as your guide: LCP two and a half seconds or less, INP two hundred milliseconds or less, CLS zero point one or less, at the seventy-fifth percentile. Fix big images, reserve space, and trim scripts. Build trust with transparent identity, pricing and terms. Persuade ethically, and never ship dark patterns, whatever a test says. Try this now. Run PageSpeed Insights on one landing page and fix one issue. Then walk your page and checkout with the dark pattern audit, and replace anything you find with an ethical alternative. | Recap and try this now<br>• Vitals: 2.5 s • 200 ms • 0.1<br>• Fix images, space, scripts<br>• Transparent trust; no dark patterns<br>• Run PageSpeed + a dark-pattern audit | Layout: recap card with three gauges and a checklist. 0-10s: recap bullets animate in; 10-20s: three gauges settle in green; 20-31s: a dark-pattern checklist ticks through; 31-41s: closing text: 'Next: landing page UX testing'. |

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Speed, trust and ethical persuasion

```text
Visitors almost never complain about a slow page, a hidden fee or a manipulative countdown timer. They just leave. Or they buy once, feel tricked, and never come back. Or they ask their bank for a chargeback. These are three silent conversion killers: speed, trust and manipulation. In this lecture, you'll learn Google's Core Web Vitals and their thresholds, how to fix the most common performance problems on marketing pages, the trust signals that make people willing to act, how to tell ethical persuasion from dark patterns, and why regulators in the US, UK and EU care about this more than ever.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because each of these problems taxes every visitor you pay for. A slow page loses people before they've read a word. Missing trust signals make ready buyers hesitate. And dark patterns might lift a short-term number, but they increase refunds, chargebacks and complaints, and damage the brand. Here's an analogy. Think of a market stall. If the queue is slow, people walk away. If the prices aren't marked, people are suspicious. And if the seller slips an extra item into your bag without asking, you'll never come back, and you'll tell your friends.
```

Scene 3 — Core Web Vitals

```text
Let's start with speed. Google's Core Web Vitals measure three aspects of experience. Largest Contentful Paint, or LCP, measures how quickly the main content appears. Good is two and a half seconds or less. Interaction to Next Paint, or INP, measures how quickly the page responds when someone taps or types. Good is two hundred milliseconds or less. INP replaced an older metric, First Input Delay, in March twenty twenty-four. And Cumulative Layout Shift, or CLS, measures visual stability. Good is zero point one or less. Google judges these at the seventy-fifth percentile of real visits, for mobile and desktop separately.
```

Scene 4 — What slows marketing pages

```text
What typically hurts these numbers on marketing pages? For LCP, huge hero images, slow servers and render-blocking scripts. For INP, heavy JavaScript and too many third-party scripts, like pixels, chat widgets and heatmaps. For CLS, images without dimensions, and banners or ads that load late and push content down, which is how people end up tapping the wrong button. Here's the key idea. Marketers often cause these problems, one tag at a time. So marketers can often fix them too, without deep engineering: compress images, reserve space, and question every script.
```

Scene 5 — Trust signals

```text
Now trust. Trust decides whether visitors are willing to act at all. The essentials: clear business identity, meaning name, location or contact details, and registration details where relevant. Real testimonials and reviews, used with consent, and not cherry-picked to mislead. Transparent pricing that includes taxes, fees and delivery. Clear refund and cancellation terms. Privacy and cookie information. Reputable payment options, including locally trusted methods. Consistent, professional, error-free design. And a responsive support channel, like chat, email or WhatsApp. None of these are exciting. All of them matter.
```

Scene 6 — Persuasion vs dark patterns

```text
Persuasion helps people make decisions that are good for them, by making benefits, proof and next steps clear. Dark patterns, also called deceptive design, trick or pressure people into things they wouldn't otherwise do. Fake urgency, like a countdown that resets when you refresh. Fake scarcity, like only two left when that isn't true. Confirmshaming, like no thanks, I don't like saving money. Sneaking items into the basket. Hidden costs that appear at the last step. Easy sign-up with very hard cancellation. Free trials that silently convert with no reminder. Pre-ticked consent. And ads disguised as content or reviews.
```

Scene 7 — The 2025–2026 regulatory picture

```text
Regulators care. Here's the picture as of twenty twenty-six, as general information, not legal advice. In the US, the FTC's click to cancel rule was struck down by an appeals court in July twenty twenty-five, and the FTC reopened rulemaking in twenty twenty-six. But the FTC still acts against subscription traps and deceptive design under existing law, and several states have their own auto-renewal laws. In the UK, the Digital Markets, Competition and Consumers Act gave the regulator direct fining powers from April twenty twenty-five, and bans fake reviews and drip pricing. In the EU, the Digital Services Act prohibits deceptive interface design on online platforms.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 1: subscription sign-up

```text
Worked example one, simple. A subscription sign-up page. Before: the price is shown as from nine dollars, with a twenty-nine dollar setup fee revealed only at checkout. The annual plan is pre-selected. Cancellation is by email only. And a timer says offer ends in ten minutes, but resets on every refresh. After: the full price and setup fee are shown upfront. Monthly and annual are a clear choice with real totals. Cancellation happens in account settings. A reminder email goes out before renewal. And the launch discount has a real end date.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example 2: electronics retailer (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. An online electronics retailer in Dubai sees a strong conversion rate but a growing number of chargebacks. The team audits the checkout and finds an extended warranty pre-added to every basket, and delivery fees that appear only on the final step. Removing the pre-added warranty and showing all-in prices early causes a small dip in conversion at first. But chargebacks and complaints fall sharply, and the average customer comes back more often. Measured over a quarter, revenue per customer improves. That's what ethical persuasion looks like in the numbers.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: speed + ethics audit

```text
Watch me do it. First, performance. I run the page through PageSpeed Insights and check the field data. LCP is slow on mobile. The largest element is the hero image, a large file. I export it in a modern format at the right sizes, add width and height, and make sure it isn't lazy-loaded, because it's the main content. Next, layout shift from a promo banner. I reserve its space with a minimum height. Then I list every script: owner, purpose, consent category, and whether it can load later. Two old pixels nobody uses get removed. Finally, I walk the checkout with the dark pattern audit.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Adding scripts and widgets without checking performance. Lazy-loading the hero image. Hiding fees to make prices look lower. Fake timers and manipulative decline buttons. Cookie banners designed to push accept all, when consent must be a genuine choice with reject as easy as accept. And judging success by one number. How do you measure it properly? Track Core Web Vitals field data for your key templates, conversion rate, and quality guardrails: refunds, chargebacks, early cancellations and complaints. Ethical persuasion shows up as steady or rising conversion with falling complaints.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Use Core Web Vitals as your guide: LCP two and a half seconds or less, INP two hundred milliseconds or less, CLS zero point one or less, at the seventy-fifth percentile. Fix big images, reserve space, and trim scripts. Build trust with transparent identity, pricing and terms. Persuade ethically, and never ship dark patterns, whatever a test says. Try this now. Run PageSpeed Insights on one landing page and fix one issue. Then walk your page and checkout with the dark pattern audit, and replace anything you find with an ethical alternative.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Landing page UX testing

```text
Here's a question that saves marketing budgets. Before you spend ten thousand dollars sending people to a landing page, wouldn't you like to know whether they'll understand it? You can find out in an afternoon, for almost nothing. And after launch, you can see exactly where real visitors get stuck. In this lecture, you'll learn which testing method answers which question, how to run five-second and first-click tests, how to read heatmaps and session recordings responsibly, how to calculate whether an A/B test is even possible with your traffic, and how to use AI to speed up analysis without inventing findings.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because a landing page is really a hypothesis. We believe this headline, this proof and this button will help this audience decide. Testing checks the hypothesis before and after you spend money on it. Here's an analogy. A restaurant doesn't put a new dish on the menu without letting a few trusted diners taste it first. And after launch, the chef watches which plates come back half-eaten. Pre-launch tests are the tasting. Behavior analytics is watching the plates. And an A/B test is serving two versions to many diners and counting which one they finish.
```

Scene 3 — Method by question

```text
Choose the method by the question. Is the offer clear at a glance? That's a five-second test. Will people click the right thing first? A first-click test. Can people complete the task, and why not? A moderated usability test with about five people per round. Which of two directions do people understand better? A comprehension test with real questions, not just which do you prefer. Where do real visitors get stuck? Heatmaps and session recordings. Where do they drop off? Funnel analytics. And does a change improve conversion at scale? That's an A/B test. Here's the key idea. The wrong method gives you confident answers to the wrong question.
```

Scene 4 — The five-second test

```text
The five-second test. Show people the first mobile screen of your page for five seconds, then hide it. Ask three questions. What is this page offering? Who is it for? What would you do next? Code the answers as correct, partly correct or wrong. As a rule of thumb for a small test like this, if fewer than about half can say what the page offers, fix the headline before you buy traffic. You can run it with tools like Lyssna or Maze, or simply over a video call. Ten to twenty people per version is plenty for a clear signal.
```

Scene 5 — First-click and preference tests

```text
The first-click test. Give people a task, like you want to know the price for three staff members, where would you click first? Then record where they click. Research on first-click testing has found that a correct first click is a strong predictor of completing the task. So it's a fast way to check your labels, your navigation and where your call to action sits. And a warning about preference tests. Asking which design do you prefer mostly measures taste. If you're comparing two directions, ask comprehension questions, like what does this cost per month, and measure who gets it right.
```

Scene 6 — Heatmaps and recordings

```text
After launch, behavior analytics. Tools like Microsoft Clarity, Hotjar and Contentsquare show heatmaps of clicks and scrolling, plus session recordings of individual visits. Look for four signals. Rage clicks, repeated clicks in one spot, often on something that looks clickable but isn't. Dead clicks, which get no response. Scroll drop-off before a key section, like pricing. And hesitation on particular form fields. Use these tools responsibly. Mask form inputs and sensitive text, get consent where it's required, mention them in your privacy notice, and don't keep recordings longer than you need.
```

Scene 7 — Can you A/B test at all?

```text
Now A slash B tests. Show different versions to randomly split groups and compare outcomes. But first, a reality check: is your traffic big enough? Small improvements need big samples. Let's take an example. Your page converts three percent, and you hope for a twenty percent relative lift, to three point six percent. You'll need roughly fourteen thousand visitors per variant. At four thousand visitors a week, that's about seven weeks. If that isn't realistic, test a bolder change, test on a higher-traffic step, or use qualitative tests plus careful before and after monitoring. And never stop a test early because one version looks ahead.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 1: yoga studio

```text
Worked example one, simple. A yoga studio's class pass page. A five-second test with ten people shows most can say it's yoga classes, but only a few notice it's for beginners, which is the whole point of the offer. The headline changes to first yoga class? Start with our beginner pass. A retest shows most people now mention beginners. Then a first-click test: find out how much the pass costs. Many people click schedule. So the team adds a visible price near the top. Two tests, one afternoon, no ad budget spent.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example 2: bootcamp (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. A coding bootcamp in Lahore plans a large paid campaign. A five-second test with twelve people shows most think the page is for a university degree. A first-click test shows people clicking curriculum when asked about price. The team names the audience and outcome in the headline, adds a visible fees and installments link, and retests. Most people now describe the offer correctly. After launch, Clarity recordings show rage clicks on a testimonial photo, so they link it to the full case study. With enough traffic, they A/B test two pricing layouts.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: sample size in Python

```text
Watch me do it. I open a terminal and run the sample size script from the lesson. It uses only Python's standard library. I enter a baseline conversion rate of three percent and a relative lift of twenty percent, with the usual five percent significance level and eighty percent power. It returns about fourteen thousand visitors per variant, and with my illustrative four thousand visitors a week, about seven weeks. Too long for this campaign. So instead, I write a test plan for a bolder change, a new headline and offer framing, and set a guardrail for refund requests. Then I set up a five-second test to check it first.
```

Scene 11 — AI-assisted synthesis

```text
One more speed-up: AI-assisted synthesis. After five moderated sessions, I paste anonymized notes into an approved AI assistant and ask for each participant's task success, where they hesitated, and verbatim quotes about confusion, then issues grouped with counts, like three out of five, and a suggested severity. The rule: no invented quotes. I check every quote and count against my notes before I share anything. AI makes the summary faster. It doesn't make it true. You do that part.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Using preference votes as evidence. Running A/B tests on low traffic and trusting noisy winners. Stopping a test the moment one version looks ahead. Testing tiny changes, like button shades, when the headline is unclear. Recording sessions without masking or consent. And testing only after launch. How do you measure success? Before launch, the share of people who answer or click correctly. After launch, conversion by device and source, with guardrails. And across your program, how many issues you catch before launch versus after. A healthy habit moves discovery earlier.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Pick the test by the question. Five-second tests for clarity. First-click tests for labels and placement. Usability tests for why. Heatmaps and recordings for where real visitors get stuck, used responsibly. And A slash B tests for impact at scale, only after you've calculated the sample size. Use AI to speed up synthesis, and verify everything. Try this now. Run a five-second test with five people on your main landing page, and a first-click test on one key task. Then run the sample size script with your own numbers, and write a test plan you can actually complete.
```

</details>

### 15. WCAG principles, levels and why they matter

Lesson `wcag-principles` · module “Accessibility essentials (WCAG basics)” · target 8 min · 12 scenes · 1158 words · planned 8:23 (words ≈ 8:23) · status: **to produce**

| Term | Say |
|---|---|
| WCAG | W-cag |
| POUR | pour |
| W3C | W three C |
| ISO | I S O |
| IEC | I E C |
| ADA | A D A |

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0:00 | 44 | Here's a number worth remembering. The World Health Organization estimates that around one point three billion people, about one in six people worldwide, experience significant disability. Now add everyone with a temporary or situational limitation. A broken arm. Bright sunlight on a phone. A noisy train with no headphones. Accessibility isn't a niche. It's a large share of your customers on any given day. In this lecture, you'll learn what the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines are, the four POUR principles, the conformance levels, what's changed in twenty twenty-five and twenty twenty-six, and how to map barriers on your most important pages. | WCAG principles, levels and why they matter<br>• About 1 in 6 people worldwide<br>• Plus situational limits<br>• POUR principles and levels<br>• 2025–2026 updates | Layout: population grid with highlighted figures. 0-11s: a grid of six person icons appears with one highlighted; 11-22s: situational icons appear: a sling, a sun, a noisy train; 22-33s: the grid expands to show many more affected people; 33-44s: the title card fades in. |
