<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — WCAG principles, levels and why they matter

```text
Here's a number worth remembering. The World Health Organization estimates that around one point three billion people, about one in six people worldwide, experience significant disability. Now add everyone with a temporary or situational limitation. A broken arm. Bright sunlight on a phone. A noisy train with no headphones. Accessibility isn't a niche. It's a large share of your customers on any given day. In this lecture, you'll learn what the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines are, the four POUR principles, the conformance levels, what's changed in twenty twenty-five and twenty twenty-six, and how to map barriers on your most important pages.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does accessibility matter for marketers? First, reach. Barriers exclude real customers. Second, usability. Accessible design is usually clearer and easier for everyone. Third, law. Accessibility is increasingly a legal requirement in many markets. And fourth, reputation. Think about curb cuts, those ramps in the sidewalk at crossings. They were designed for wheelchair users. But parents with strollers, travelers with suitcases and delivery workers with trolleys use them every day. Accessible design works the same way. Captions help people watching on mute. Good contrast helps people in sunlight. Clear errors help everyone.
```

Scene 3 — The standard

```text
The Web Content Accessibility Guidelines, WCAG, are published by the W3C and are the internationally recognized standard. WCAG two point two, published in twenty twenty-three, is the current version. It builds on two point zero and two point one, so meeting two point two generally satisfies the earlier versions too. And here's a twenty twenty-five update. In October twenty twenty-five, WCAG two point two was approved as an ISO standard, ISO slash IEC forty five hundred, colon twenty twenty-five, with identical content. That makes it easier for governments and big buyers to require it. WCAG three is still a draft, not a standard, so keep targeting two point two.
```

Scene 4 — The four POUR principles

```text
WCAG is organized around four principles, which spell POUR. Perceivable: information can be perceived through at least one sense. Think alt text, captions, sufficient contrast, and text that can be resized. Operable: the interface can be used in different ways, including by keyboard, with enough time, without seizure-inducing flashes, and with targets big enough to hit. Understandable: content and behavior make sense, with clear language, predictable interactions and helpful error messages. Robust: it works with current and future technologies, including assistive technology like screen readers, through valid, semantic code with proper names and roles for controls.
```

Scene 5 — Conformance levels

```text
Now conformance levels. Level A is the minimum. Failing it creates severe barriers. Level AA is the level most laws, policies and organizations target. It includes contrast of four point five to one for normal text, captions for prerecorded video, visible focus, and more. Level AAA is enhanced, and usually isn't required for entire sites, because some criteria can't be met for all content. So here's the practical rule for your team. Target WCAG two point two, Level AA. Put that exact sentence into every brief you write.
```

Scene 6 — The legal picture (general info)

```text
What's the legal picture? It varies by market, so treat this as general information. In the European Union, the European Accessibility Act has applied since June twenty-eighth, twenty twenty-five, to many consumer products and services, including e-commerce, usually assessed against a European standard that incorporates WCAG. In the UK, the Equality Act requires reasonable adjustments, and public sector bodies have specific website rules. In the US, private businesses face lawsuits under the ADA, and a federal rule requires state and local governments to meet WCAG two point one AA, with deadlines extended in April twenty twenty-six to twenty twenty-seven and twenty twenty-eight. Gulf markets have disability rights laws and government digital policies too.
```

Scene 7 — About overlays

```text
A warning about accessibility overlays. Some vendors sell widgets that claim to make your site compliant automatically with one line of code. Accessibility experts and disabled users widely criticize them. They often fail to fix underlying problems, and they can interfere with the assistive technology people already use. An overlay is like putting a ramp sticker on a staircase. It looks like you've done something, but nobody can get up the stairs. Fix the real design, content and code instead.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 1: creator course site

```text
Worked example one, simple. A creator's course website gets six quick wins in one afternoon. Gray body text is darkened to reach four point five to one. Instructor and course images get meaningful alt text, and decorative images are marked as decorative. The sales video gets reviewed captions. Click here links become descriptive, like see the full curriculum. The cookie banner and sticky header are adjusted so they don't hide keyboard focus. And tiny social icons in the footer are enlarged to meet the target size guidance. None of these changes the brand. All of them widen the audience.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example 2: EU-facing store (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. A UK online homeware store sells to customers across the EU. After the European Accessibility Act takes effect, the team maps barriers on its three most important pages: a campaign landing page, product pages and checkout. They find pale text on promotional banners, a checkout step where a custom dropdown has no accessible name, error messages that only turn fields red, and a cookie banner that traps keyboard users. They fix the highest-severity items first, add WCAG two point two AA to every agency brief, and publish an accessibility statement with a contact route.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: POUR barrier map

```text
Watch me do it. I open a POUR barrier map for the pricing page. Perceivable: I run a contrast checker on the gray plan descriptions. Three point one to one. That fails. Operable: I put the mouse away and press tab. I reach the plan toggle, but I can't operate it with the keyboard. Understandable: I trigger an error in the sign-up form. It says invalid input. Robust: I open the browser's accessibility pane and inspect the custom dropdown. It has no name. Four barriers in ten minutes. I note who each affects, a fix, and the effort, then sort by severity.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Treating accessibility as a final checklist item instead of a design requirement. Relying on overlays. Assuming few of our users are disabled, when many disabilities are invisible and people don't announce them. Ignoring situational limitations like bright sunlight or noise. And not writing accessibility into briefs, so agencies and freelancers never deliver it. How do you measure progress? Open accessibility issues by severity on key templates. The share of new pages that pass accessibility QA before launch. And feedback through your accessibility contact.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Accessibility affects a large share of your audience. WCAG two point two is the current standard, and it's now also an ISO standard. It's organized around POUR: perceivable, operable, understandable and robust. Target level AA. Legal requirements are growing, from the EU's accessibility act to ADA lawsuits and public sector rules. And fix real issues instead of relying on overlays. Try this now. Pick your three most important pages for conversions. For each, find one likely barrier under each POUR principle, and choose three to fix this month.
```

</details>

| 1 | 0:00 | 46 | Here's a test you can try right now. Open a landing page with a cookie banner, a sticky call to action bar and a chat bubble. Put your mouse away and press the tab key. Watch the focus move down the page. At some point, it vanishes. It's still there, but it's hidden underneath the banner or the chat bubble, and a keyboard user has no idea where they are. That exact problem became a named requirement in WCAG two point two. In this lecture, you'll learn the new criteria that matter most for marketing pages, and exactly how to fix the four most common failures. | WCAG 2.2 in practice<br>• Press Tab and watch focus vanish<br>• New criteria that hit marketing pages<br>• Fix the four most common failures | Layout: landing page with sticky elements and a moving focus ring. 0-12s: a cookie banner, a sticky CTA bar and a chat bubble appear; 12-23s: a focus ring moves down the page with each Tab press; 23-34s: the ring disappears beneath the sticky bar; 34-46s: the title card fades in. |
| 2 | 0:46 | 41 | Why focus on two point two specifically? Because it targets the patterns marketers love. Sticky bars. Chat widgets. Tiny icon buttons. Image sliders. Multi-step forms. Login screens. And because WCAG two point two is now referenced widely, including as ISO standard forty five hundred colon twenty twenty-five, it's increasingly what contracts, procurement and regulators point to. Think of it like a building code update. The old code covered doors and stairs. The update adds rules for the new things everyone started building: glass walls, automatic doors, rooftop terraces. Same principles, applied to modern designs. | Why 2.2 matters to marketers<br>• Targets sticky bars, chat, icons, sliders<br>• Forms and logins<br>• Referenced in contracts and standards<br>• A building-code update | Layout: building code analogy. 0-10s: a classic building appears with doors and stairs; 10-20s: modern additions appear: glass walls, automatic doors; 20-31s: new code tags attach to the modern parts; 31-41s: the building morphs into a web page with sticky bars and chat. |
| 3 | 1:27 | 41 | WCAG two point two added nine success criteria and removed one. Six of the nine are at Level A or AA, the levels most teams target. At AA: focus not obscured, minimum. Dragging movements. Target size, minimum. And accessible authentication, minimum. At Level A: consistent help, and redundant entry. The other three are at AAA: an enhanced focus not obscured, focus appearance, and enhanced accessible authentication. And the one removed is four point one point one, parsing, which modern browsers and assistive technology made unnecessary. Let's take the ones that hit marketing pages hardest. | What changed<br>• +9 criteria, 1 removed<br>• AA: Focus Not Obscured, Dragging, Target Size, Authentication<br>• A: Consistent Help, Redundant Entry<br>• Removed: 4.1.1 Parsing | Layout: criteria cards sorted by level. 0-10s: nine cards appear in a stack; 10-20s: six cards slide into A and AA columns; 20-31s: three move into a faded AAA column; 31-41s: a 4.1.1 card slides out with a 'removed' stamp. |
| 4 | 2:08 | 47 | First, focus not obscured, minimum, criterion two point four point eleven. When an element receives keyboard focus, it must not be entirely hidden by content you created, like a sticky header, a cookie banner or a chat bubble. The fix has two parts. In your CSS, add scroll padding at the top and bottom of the page, equal to the height of your sticky elements. That tells the browser to leave room when it scrolls a focused element into view. Then give focus a strong, visible style, like a three pixel outline with good contrast. And position chat bubbles and banners so they don't cover focused content. | 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured (AA)<br>• Focused element never fully hidden<br>• scroll-padding for sticky header and bar<br>• Strong :focus-visible outline<br>• Place banners and chat carefully | Layout: code panel beside a page preview. 0-12s: the CSS lines for scroll padding highlight; 12-24s: the page preview scrolls and the focused link stops above the sticky bar; 24-35s: a thick focus outline appears; 35-47s: the chat bubble moves to a non-overlapping position. |
| 5 | 2:55 | 43 | Second, target size, minimum, criterion two point five point eight. Interactive targets should be at least twenty-four by twenty-four CSS pixels, or have enough space around them. There are exceptions, such as links inside a sentence, or an equivalent control elsewhere that meets the size. The usual failures on marketing pages are tiny social icons, little close buttons on pop-ups, and carousel dots. The fix is often invisible. Enlarge the clickable area with a minimum width and height, without changing how the icon looks. And for your key actions, go bigger. Around forty-four pixels tall is comfortable for thumbs. | 2.5.8 Target Size (AA)<br>• At least 24 × 24 CSS px, or spacing<br>• Exceptions: inline links, equivalents<br>• Fails: social icons, close ×, carousel dots<br>• Bigger hit area, same look | Layout: magnified icons with target overlays. 0-11s: tiny social icons appear with a 24 by 24 overlay showing they're too small; 11-22s: the clickable area expands while the icon stays the same size; 22-32s: a close button grows its hit area; 32-43s: a primary button measures 44 px tall. |
| 6 | 3:38 | 45 | Third, dragging movements, criterion two point five point seven. Anything you can do by dragging must also be possible with a single pointer, without dragging. Think of before and after image sliders, price range sliders, and drag to reorder lists. Some people can't hold and drag, because of tremors or because they use a head pointer or voice control. The fix: add a tap alternative. Before and after buttons on the comparison slider. Number inputs beside a price range. Move up and move down buttons on a reorderable list. Keep the drag for people who like it. Just don't make it the only way. | 2.5.7 Dragging Movements (AA)<br>• Dragging needs a single-pointer alternative<br>• Sliders, ranges, drag-to-reorder<br>• Add buttons, inputs or taps | Layout: image comparison slider. 0-11s: a hand drags a before/after slider handle; 11-22s: a 'no drag' icon appears for a user with a head pointer; 22-34s: 'Before' and 'After' buttons appear beneath the slider; 34-45s: a tap switches the image instantly. |
| 7 | 4:23 | 44 | Fourth, the form and login criteria. Consistent help, at Level A: if you offer help, like a contact link, chat or FAQ, put it in the same relative place across the pages of a process. Redundant entry, also Level A: don't make people type information they've already given in the same process. Offer billing same as shipping, or let them select earlier answers. And accessible authentication, minimum, at AA: logging in shouldn't depend on a memory or puzzle test without an alternative. In practice, allow paste in password fields, support password managers, and consider passkeys or email magic links. Avoid puzzle-only CAPTCHAs. | Forms, help and logins<br>• 3.2.6 Consistent Help (A)<br>• 3.3.7 Redundant Entry (A)<br>• 3.3.8 Accessible Authentication (AA)<br>• Allow paste; password managers; passkeys | Layout: three mini screens. 0-11s: a help link sits in the same corner across three pages; 11-22s: a checkout shows 'Billing same as shipping' ticked; 22-33s: a password field accepts a pasted password from a manager; 33-44s: a puzzle CAPTCHA is crossed out. |
| 8 | 5:07 | 38 | Worked example one, simple. A newsletter pop-up. The close button is a sixteen pixel cross. Keyboard focus lands behind the pop-up instead of inside it. And the pop-up covers the focused link beneath. The fixes: enlarge the close button's target to at least twenty-four pixels, move focus into the pop-up when it opens, let escape close it, and return focus to where the person was. Four small changes to a single component, and the pop-up now passes several criteria at once, including focus not obscured and target size. | Worked example 1: newsletter pop-up<br>• 16 px close button<br>• Focus lands behind the pop-up<br>• Fix: bigger target, focus inside, Esc closes<br>• Return focus afterward | Layout: pop-up component zoomed in. 0-10s: a small close cross shows a 16 px measurement; 10-19s: a focus ring appears behind the pop-up in red; 19-28s: focus jumps inside the pop-up and the close target grows; 28-38s: pressing Esc closes it and focus returns to the page. |

| 12 | 8:01 | 47 | Let's recap. WCAG two point two added nine criteria and removed parsing. The ones that hit marketing pages hardest: focus not obscured, target size, dragging movements, consistent help, redundant entry and accessible authentication. Use scroll padding and strong focus styles for sticky elements. Enlarge hit areas to at least twenty-four pixels. Add tap alternatives to drag. Keep help in one place. Don't make people retype. And allow paste and password managers. Try this now. Take one campaign page. Tab through it with every sticky element showing, measure the small targets, find any drag-only controls, and log in or check out with a password manager. Fix or ticket every failure. | Recap and try this now<br>• Six A/AA criteria to know<br>• Scroll padding + focus styles<br>• 24 px targets • tap alternatives<br>• Consistent help • no re-typing • allow paste<br>• Run the 2.2 checklist on one page | Layout: recap card with six criteria icons. 0-12s: recap bullets animate in; 12-24s: six criteria icons light up; 24-35s: a checklist thumbnail ticks through; 35-47s: closing text: 'Next: an accessibility checklist for marketers'. |

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — WCAG 2.2 in practice

```text
Here's a test you can try right now. Open a landing page with a cookie banner, a sticky call to action bar and a chat bubble. Put your mouse away and press the tab key. Watch the focus move down the page. At some point, it vanishes. It's still there, but it's hidden underneath the banner or the chat bubble, and a keyboard user has no idea where they are. That exact problem became a named requirement in WCAG two point two. In this lecture, you'll learn the new criteria that matter most for marketing pages, and exactly how to fix the four most common failures.
```

Scene 2 — Why 2.2 matters to marketers

```text
Why focus on two point two specifically? Because it targets the patterns marketers love. Sticky bars. Chat widgets. Tiny icon buttons. Image sliders. Multi-step forms. Login screens. And because WCAG two point two is now referenced widely, including as ISO standard forty five hundred colon twenty twenty-five, it's increasingly what contracts, procurement and regulators point to. Think of it like a building code update. The old code covered doors and stairs. The update adds rules for the new things everyone started building: glass walls, automatic doors, rooftop terraces. Same principles, applied to modern designs.
```

Scene 3 — What changed

```text
WCAG two point two added nine success criteria and removed one. Six of the nine are at Level A or AA, the levels most teams target. At AA: focus not obscured, minimum. Dragging movements. Target size, minimum. And accessible authentication, minimum. At Level A: consistent help, and redundant entry. The other three are at AAA: an enhanced focus not obscured, focus appearance, and enhanced accessible authentication. And the one removed is four point one point one, parsing, which modern browsers and assistive technology made unnecessary. Let's take the ones that hit marketing pages hardest.
```

Scene 4 — 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured (AA)

```text
First, focus not obscured, minimum, criterion two point four point eleven. When an element receives keyboard focus, it must not be entirely hidden by content you created, like a sticky header, a cookie banner or a chat bubble. The fix has two parts. In your CSS, add scroll padding at the top and bottom of the page, equal to the height of your sticky elements. That tells the browser to leave room when it scrolls a focused element into view. Then give focus a strong, visible style, like a three pixel outline with good contrast. And position chat bubbles and banners so they don't cover focused content.
```

Scene 5 — 2.5.8 Target Size (AA)

```text
Second, target size, minimum, criterion two point five point eight. Interactive targets should be at least twenty-four by twenty-four CSS pixels, or have enough space around them. There are exceptions, such as links inside a sentence, or an equivalent control elsewhere that meets the size. The usual failures on marketing pages are tiny social icons, little close buttons on pop-ups, and carousel dots. The fix is often invisible. Enlarge the clickable area with a minimum width and height, without changing how the icon looks. And for your key actions, go bigger. Around forty-four pixels tall is comfortable for thumbs.
```

Scene 6 — 2.5.7 Dragging Movements (AA)

```text
Third, dragging movements, criterion two point five point seven. Anything you can do by dragging must also be possible with a single pointer, without dragging. Think of before and after image sliders, price range sliders, and drag to reorder lists. Some people can't hold and drag, because of tremors or because they use a head pointer or voice control. The fix: add a tap alternative. Before and after buttons on the comparison slider. Number inputs beside a price range. Move up and move down buttons on a reorderable list. Keep the drag for people who like it. Just don't make it the only way.
```

Scene 7 — Forms, help and logins

```text
Fourth, the form and login criteria. Consistent help, at Level A: if you offer help, like a contact link, chat or FAQ, put it in the same relative place across the pages of a process. Redundant entry, also Level A: don't make people type information they've already given in the same process. Offer billing same as shipping, or let them select earlier answers. And accessible authentication, minimum, at AA: logging in shouldn't depend on a memory or puzzle test without an alternative. In practice, allow paste in password fields, support password managers, and consider passkeys or email magic links. Avoid puzzle-only CAPTCHAs.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 1: newsletter pop-up

```text
Worked example one, simple. A newsletter pop-up. The close button is a sixteen pixel cross. Keyboard focus lands behind the pop-up instead of inside it. And the pop-up covers the focused link beneath. The fixes: enlarge the close button's target to at least twenty-four pixels, move focus into the pop-up when it opens, let escape close it, and return focus to where the person was. Four small changes to a single component, and the pop-up now passes several criteria at once, including focus not obscured and target size.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example 2: Black Friday page (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. A retailer's Black Friday page has a sticky countdown bar, a floating chat bubble, a before and after drag slider, tiny close buttons on promo cards, and a checkout that blocks pasting the discount code. Running the checklist: focus disappears under the sticky bar and chat bubble. The slider is drag-only. The close buttons are sixteen pixels. And customers must retype a code from an email. The fixes: scroll padding and a repositioned chat bubble, previous and next buttons on the slider, larger close targets, and paste allowed. The page looks almost identical. It just works for far more people.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: fix and re-test

```text
Watch me do it. I open the campaign page and press tab from the top. Focus disappears under the sticky bar at the fourth link. I open the stylesheet and add scroll padding at the top for the header and at the bottom for the bar, and a three pixel focus visible outline. I tab again. The focused link now stops above the bar. Next, I measure the footer social icons: eighteen pixels. I add a minimum width and height of twenty-four pixels to their links. Then I check the slider, add before and after buttons, and try checking out with a password manager. Paste works. I tick each line on the checklist.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Adding a sticky bar without tabbing through the page. Shrinking icons to look minimal, so they fail target size. Launching drag-only sliders. Moving the help link to different places on each page. Blocking paste in password or code fields for security theater. And relying on puzzle CAPTCHAs. How do you measure success? Add the WCAG two point two checklist to your pre-launch QA and track the pass rate for new campaign pages. Watch keyboard-only task completion in your testing. And look for fewer complaints about logins, checkouts and pop-ups.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. WCAG two point two added nine criteria and removed parsing. The ones that hit marketing pages hardest: focus not obscured, target size, dragging movements, consistent help, redundant entry and accessible authentication. Use scroll padding and strong focus styles for sticky elements. Enlarge hit areas to at least twenty-four pixels. Add tap alternatives to drag. Keep help in one place. Don't make people retype. And allow paste and password managers. Try this now. Take one campaign page. Tab through it with every sticky element showing, measure the small targets, find any drag-only controls, and log in or check out with a password manager. Fix or ticket every failure.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — An accessibility checklist for marketers

```text
Here's something that surprises most marketers. Many of the biggest accessibility barriers on the web aren't in the code. They're in the content. An image with no description. A video with wrong captions. A button that says click here. Gray text on a pale background. An email that's one giant picture. And who creates that content? Marketers do. In this lecture, you'll get a practical accessibility checklist you can apply without being a developer, covering images, video, text structure, color, forms, keyboard use, documents, emails and social posts, plus how to use AI for alt text without getting it wrong.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because content is where accessibility is won or lost every single day. Developers might build an accessible template once. But marketers publish new images, videos, emails and posts constantly. If each piece adds a barrier, the site slowly becomes inaccessible again. Think of a well-built library with ramps, lifts and wide doors. If staff keep piling boxes in the aisles, the building's design doesn't matter anymore. Your content habits are the aisles. Keep them clear, and everything else keeps working.
```

Scene 3 — Images

```text
Start with images. Meaningful images need alt text that describes the content and its purpose, concisely. Instructor demonstrating a video edit on a laptop. Don't start with image of, because screen readers already announce it's an image. Decorative images get empty alt text, so screen readers skip them. Avoid images of text. If you can't, like on a social graphic, put the text in the alt text or caption. For charts and infographics, add a short text summary of the key points. The formula: what it is, plus the key detail relevant to the context, plus any text in the image.
```

Scene 4 — Video and audio

```text
Video and audio. Every prerecorded video with sound needs captions at Level AA. Auto-captions are a starting point, not an ending. Review and correct them, especially names, brands and mixed-language speech, like English mixed with Urdu or Arabic. Provide transcripts for podcasts and audio. Where important information is only visual, add audio description or descriptive narration. Never autoplay with sound, and always provide controls. And avoid flashing content. No more than three flashes in any one second, because flashing can trigger seizures.
```

Scene 5 — Text, structure and color

```text
Text and structure. Use headings in order, one heading level one, then level two, then three, describing each section, because screen reader users navigate by headings. Make links descriptive. Download the pricing guide, PDF, not click here. Write in plain language. Mark up lists as real lists. Set the page language correctly, which matters on multilingual sites so screen readers pronounce Arabic, Urdu or English properly. Then color and contrast. Text needs at least four point five to one, or three to one for large text. Buttons and meaningful graphics need three to one. And never use color alone to convey meaning.
```

Scene 6 — Forms and keyboard

```text
Forms and keyboard. Every field needs a visible label that's properly connected to it. Put instructions before the form, not only in placeholders. Errors must be described in text, say how to fix them, and be announced to assistive technology. Don't set tight time limits without a way to extend. And allow pasting, including into password fields. For keyboard users, everything must work with tab, shift and tab, enter, space and arrow keys. Focus must be visible, move in a logical order, and pop-ups should trap focus while open, close with escape, and return focus when they close.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: email campaign

```text
Worked example one, simple. An email campaign. Before: a single large image containing all the text, a click here link, and light gray footer text. After: live text with real headings, a hero image with alt text, a button that says shop the Ramadan collection, higher contrast in the footer, and a plain text version. The team checks it with a screen reader and in dark mode. Here's why it matters. When images are blocked, which many email clients do by default, the old email showed nothing at all. The new one still works.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: 2,000 product images (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. An online fashion brand in Karachi has two thousand product images and no alt text. Writing it all by hand isn't realistic this quarter. So the team uses an AI assistant to draft alt text in batches, with strict rules: under a hundred and twenty-five characters, describe what matters to a shopper, don't guess fabrics or brands that aren't visible, and reply decorative if it is. A merchandiser reviews every draft. About one in ten gets corrected, usually a wrong color or an invented detail. Top-selling products go first.
```

Scene 9 — Watch me do it: alt text + captions

```text
Watch me do it. I open a product image in our store's editor and find the alt text field. I paste the image into an approved AI assistant with the context: product listing for a women's linen shirt on a category page. Rules: under a hundred and twenty-five characters, describe what a shopper needs, no guessing. It returns: woman in a white linen shirt with rolled sleeves and a relaxed fit. I check the image. The shirt is actually off-white, cream. I correct it, paste it in, and save. Then I open the product video, download the auto-captions, fix the brand name, and upload the corrected caption file.
```

Scene 10 — Social and documents

```text
A few quick wins on social and documents. On social platforms, use the alt text option when you post images. Write multi-word hashtags in camel case, capitalizing each word, so screen readers can pronounce them. Put important text in the caption as well as in the image. And avoid decorative Unicode fonts, which screen readers may read letter by letter or skip completely. For documents, prefer an accessible web page to a PDF. If you must publish a PDF, export from a tool that keeps headings and alt text, and run its accessibility checker first.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Missing or useless alt text, like image one two three dot jpg. Uncorrected auto-captions. Choosing heading levels for visual size instead of structure. Emails built entirely as images. Color as the only signal for errors or sale prices. And AI-drafted alt text published without review. How do you measure success? Add an accessibility sign-off to every campaign launch. Then track the share of campaigns that pass it before launch, the share of images on key pages with meaningful alt text, and the share of videos with reviewed captions.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Marketers control many accessibility factors. Write concise, purposeful alt text, and mark decorative images as decorative. Caption and review every video. Use logical headings and descriptive links. Check contrast, and never rely on color alone. Give forms visible labels and clear, announced errors. Make sure everything works with a keyboard. Build emails from live text. And use AI to draft, never to publish unreviewed. Try this now. Run the accessibility quick check on one landing page and one email. Fix the alt text, links and contrast issues you find, and correct the captions on one video.
```

</details>

Run them on your key templates, and after major changes. If you have a developer, you can build it into your launch checklist with Playwright and the open source axe-core engine. The script in the lesson scans a list of pages and prints each violation with its impact and the number of affected elements. | Layer 1: automated checks<br>• axe DevTools • WAVE • Lighthouse<br>• Flags alt, contrast, labels, empty links, ARIA, language<br>• Automate with Playwright + axe-core | Layout: browser with an extension panel. 0-10s: an extension panel lists violations by impact; 10-20s: a contrast issue highlights on the page; 20-31s: a code editor shows the Playwright scan script; 31-41s: a terminal prints violations with counts. |

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Testing accessibility

```text
An automated accessibility scan shows zero errors. The team celebrates. Then someone tries to book an appointment using only a keyboard, and gets stuck on the date picker. A screen reader announces every time slot as just button, button, button. The scan was right about what it checked. It simply can't check everything. In this lecture, you'll learn the four layers of accessibility testing: automated scans, manual checks anyone can do, basic screen reader testing, and testing with disabled users. Plus how to automate a scan in your launch checklist and publish an accessibility statement.
```

Scene 2 — Why layers?

```text
Why do you need layers? Because automated tools can detect only a portion of WCAG issues. They can tell you an image has no alt attribute. They can't tell you whether the alt text makes sense. They can flag low contrast. They can't tell you whether your checkout is understandable. Think of it like a car inspection. A machine can measure the tire pressure and the emissions. But you still need someone to drive the car and notice the steering pulls to the left. Automated scans are the machine. Manual and user testing are the test drive.
```

Scene 3 — Layer 1: automated checks

```text
Layer one: automated checks. Browser extensions like axe DevTools and WAVE, and the accessibility section of a Lighthouse report, flag missing alt attributes, low contrast text, missing form labels, empty links or buttons, incorrect ARIA usage and a missing page language. Run them on your key templates, and after major changes. If you have a developer, you can build it into your launch checklist with Playwright and the open source axe-core engine. The script in the lesson scans a list of pages and prints each violation with its impact and the number of affected elements.
```

Scene 4 — Layer 2: manual checks

```text
Layer two: manual checks anyone can do. First, the ten minute keyboard test. Put the mouse away. Press tab from the top. Can you always see where focus is? Can you reach and operate every link, button, menu, form field, accordion and pop-up? Is the order logical? Can you close pop-ups with escape and return to where you were? Is focus ever hidden behind sticky elements? Then zoom the browser to two hundred and four hundred percent. Does content reflow into one column without horizontal scrolling? Then spot-check contrast on text over images, and turn on reduce motion to see if animations calm down.
```

Scene 5 — Layer 3: screen reader basics

```text
Layer three: screen reader basics. Screen readers read the page aloud and let people jump by headings, links, landmarks and form fields. You already have one. VoiceOver is built into Apple devices. TalkBack is built into Android. Narrator is built into Windows, and NVDA is free for Windows. A basic test: turn it on and listen from the top. Jump by headings. Do they outline the page? Jump by links. Do they make sense out of context? Complete the main form. Are labels, errors and the confirmation announced? It feels awkward at first. Remember, experienced users are much faster than you. Even so, your first test will reveal major issues.
```

Scene 6 — Layer 4: disabled users

```text
Layer four: testing with disabled users. Nothing replaces feedback from people who use assistive technology every day. Include disabled participants in your usability tests, recruited through disability organizations or specialist recruitment services, and pay them fairly for their time and expertise. Offer a feedback route in an accessibility statement. And for major launches or regulated contexts, commission an expert audit. Here's the key idea. Your own screen reader test tells you about obvious barriers. A skilled daily user tells you what it's actually like to use your site.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: booking widget

```text
Worked example one, simple. A booking widget. The automated scan shows zero errors. The keyboard test reveals the date picker can't be used without a mouse, and focus disappears when the booking pop-up opens. The screen reader test finds every time slot announced only as button. The fixes: a keyboard-operable date picker, or a simple date input as an alternative. Focus moved into the pop-up and kept there while it's open. And accessible names for each slot, like book three p m, Thursday June twelfth. Then a disabled tester confirms the flow works.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: training provider (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. A government-funded training provider in the US must meet WCAG two point one AA for its public courses under the ADA Title two rule, and it wants to reach two point two anyway. The digital team adds the Playwright and axe scan to every release, runs a keyboard and zoom check on each new template, and books quarterly sessions with three disabled testers through a disability organization. The first round finds a registration form where errors aren't announced, and a video player without caption controls. Both are fixed before the compliance date.
```

Scene 9 — Watch me do it: three layers

```text
Watch me do it. In a terminal, I install Playwright and the axe integration, then run the scan script with two URLs from an environment variable: the homepage and the pricing page. It reports two violations on pricing. A serious contrast failure on six elements, and a critical missing form label on one. I log them. Then I put the mouse away and tab through pricing. The plan toggle can't be reached. The scan didn't catch that. Finally, I turn on VoiceOver, jump by headings, and hear two headings called untitled. Three layers, three different kinds of problems.
```

Scene 10 — Accessibility statement + workflow

```text
Finally, publish an accessibility statement. It explains your commitment, the standard you aim for, like WCAG two point two AA, when you last reviewed the site, how you test, known limitations, and how people can contact you for help or to report problems, with a response time. It's required for some public sector sites and good practice for everyone. And build accessibility into your workflow: in the brief, in design, in content, in build, in QA before launch, and in ongoing monitoring. Accessibility that's checked once decays. Accessibility that's part of the process lasts.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Treating a clean automated scan as proof of accessibility. Never testing with a keyboard. Using third-party widgets like chat, booking or cookie banners without checking their accessibility. Having no way for users to report problems. And testing once before launch, then never again. How do you measure success? Automated violations per key template, trending toward zero on new pages. Keyboard and screen reader task success in your manual tests. Issues found by disabled testers. And how quickly you respond to accessibility feedback.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Layer your testing. Automated scans catch some issues fast. Manual keyboard, zoom and contrast checks catch many more. Basic screen reader tests reveal how your page actually sounds. And disabled users tell you what it's really like. Publish an accessibility statement, and build checks into every stage. Try this now. Do a keyboard test and a two hundred percent zoom test on your key page. Then try one task with your phone's built-in screen reader. Record three issues, and draft a short accessibility statement using the template.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Running lightweight usability tests

```text
Here's the fastest way to humble a marketing team. Sit them down and let them watch one real customer try to use their website. Just one. Within five minutes, someone will whisper, but it's right there! And someone else will realize the customer doesn't know what our key product term even means. Usability testing is watching real people try to complete real tasks. It's the most reliable way to learn why people struggle, and it doesn't need a lab or a big budget. In this lecture, you'll learn how many people to test, how to plan and write tasks, how to facilitate, recruit ethically, and turn observations into fixes.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because analytics can tell you that sixty percent of mobile visitors leave at step two. It can't tell you why. Usability testing tells you why. Maybe they can't find the delivery date. Maybe they think the price is per month when it's per year. Here's an analogy. Analytics is a smoke alarm. It tells you there's a problem somewhere in the building. Usability testing is walking into the room and seeing what's actually burning. You need both, but only one tells you what to fix.
```

Scene 3 — How many participants?

```text
How many people? A widely cited guideline from usability research is that testing with around five users per round reveals many of the most common problems, especially for one fairly similar audience. The key idea is small, repeated rounds, not one big study. Test five, fix what you find, then test again. If you have distinct audiences, like customers in different countries or languages, test a few people from each group. Five people is enough to find problems. It isn't enough to measure percentages precisely, so don't report it as a statistic.
```

Scene 4 — Plan the test

```text
Planning. Write a one-page plan. The goal: what do we want to learn, like can mobile users book a consultation. The prototype or page, and the device. Participants: who, how many, how you'll recruit them, and the incentive. Three to five realistic tasks. The metrics: task success, time, errors and a confidence or ease rating. The method, moderated or unmoderated, remote or in person. Consent and data handling. And the schedule. Sessions of twenty to forty-five minutes are typical. Then write a screener, a short set of questions to confirm people match your audience without revealing the right answers.
```

Scene 5 — Writing good tasks

```text
Writing good tasks is where most tests succeed or fail. Tasks should be realistic scenarios that don't give away the answer. Poor: click the pricing link in the menu. Better: you're considering this service for your salon, find out how much it would cost for three staff members. Notice the better version doesn't use the word pricing. If your task uses the exact words on the interface, you're testing whether people can match words, not whether they understand your site. Give people a reason, a context, and a goal, and then let them find their own way.
```

Scene 6 — Facilitating well

```text
Facilitating well. Ask participants to think aloud, saying what they're thinking as they go. Stay neutral. Don't help, and don't defend the design. If someone asks should I tap here, answer, what would you do if I weren't here? Probe gently: what did you expect to happen? What made you choose that? Focus on behavior over opinions. What people do matters more than I like it. And take structured notes, ideally with a second person as note-taker, so you can focus on the participant. The hardest part for most marketers is silence. Let people struggle for a moment. That's where the learning is.
```

Scene 7 — Recruit ethically

```text
Recruiting, ethically. Good sources: existing customers, email subscribers and social followers, screened to match your audience. Paid recruitment services for specific profiles. Friends and colleagues only for very early checks, because they aren't your audience, and they'll be polite. Offer a fair incentive, like a voucher or payment, and respect people's time. Get informed consent for recording, and explain how you'll store and use the data, in line with privacy laws. And include people with disabilities and a range of devices when you can. Moderated or unmoderated? Beginners should start with moderated remote tests over video calls.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 1: salon software

```text
Worked example one, simple. A salon booking software company tests its pricing page with five salon owners over video calls. Task one: find out what it would cost for three staff members. Four of five can't work it out. One says, is this per salon or per user? Two people scroll past the book a demo button because a sticky banner covers it on their phones. Two ask what happens if it doesn't work for them. The fixes: a worked price example for three staff, the button moved higher with better contrast, and a refund FAQ right beside pricing.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example 2: tutoring service (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. An online tutoring service in Karachi tests its sign-up flow with five parents on their own phones, over video calls. Three findings. Parents don't understand the difference between two plans. Most miss the free trial class option entirely. And for one parent, the WhatsApp contact button opens a broken link inside a social app's browser. The team fixes all three within a week: plan names based on outcomes with a one-line comparison, the free trial as the primary button, and a working WhatsApp link. A second round with four parents confirms the improvements.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: remote moderated test

```text
Watch me do it. I send a four-question screener to newsletter subscribers and book five thirty-minute calls. In each session, I read the intro script: we're testing the website, not you, please think aloud, may I record? I wait for a clear yes. I give task one as a scenario. After each task, I ask how easy was that, from one to five. My colleague fills in the note sheet: participant, task, time, observation, verbatim quote, success and a severity guess. Within an hour of the last session, we list the top three issues. Then I paste the anonymized notes into an approved AI assistant for a first summary, and verify every quote against the recordings.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Testing with colleagues instead of target users. Leading tasks that give away the answer. Helping participants the moment they hesitate. Reporting five-person results as percentages. And the worst: collecting findings and never acting on them. How do you measure success? Compare rounds. The same tasks after fixes should show higher success, fewer high-severity issues and better ease ratings. Then look for the matching live signals: higher completion on the relevant step, and fewer support questions on the same topic.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Watch real people try realistic tasks. Run small rounds of about five participants, fix what you find, and test again. Write scenario tasks that don't give away the answer. Stay neutral, use think aloud, and focus on behavior. Recruit representative people ethically, with consent and fair incentives. And turn every observation into a prioritized fix. Try this now. Write a test plan with three scenario tasks for one of your pages. Recruit three to five people from your audience, run the sessions remotely, and complete a findings table with severity and fixes.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Analytics, heatmaps and A/B testing

```text
A founder shows you a dashboard. Conversion went up four percent after the redesign! Great, you say. Compared to when? Was it the redesign, or the sale that started the same week? Did mobile improve, or only desktop? And did refunds go up too? Silence. Data is powerful, but only when you ask it the right questions. In this lecture, you'll learn the key analytics for UX, how to set up useful tracking in GA4 with privacy in mind, how to use heatmaps and session recordings responsibly, how A/B tests work, their pitfalls, when not to test, and how to combine evidence into a reliable improvement cycle.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Usability testing tells you why people struggle. Analytics tells you what happens at scale. Where people come from, where they drop off, what they click. You need both. Think of analytics like a map of traffic flow in a city. It shows you which roads are jammed and which exits nobody takes. It doesn't tell you that a sign is missing at the junction. For that, you go and stand at the junction, which is usability testing. Put the two together and you know where the problem is and why it happens.
```

Scene 3 — Key metrics for UX

```text
Here are the key metrics for UX. Conversion rate, the share of visitors who complete the goal, which depends heavily on traffic quality. Funnel drop-off, which step loses the most people. Engagement and scroll depth, how far people read. Remember, scrolling isn't understanding. Device and browser split, because problems are often device-specific. Check in-app browsers separately. Traffic source performance, though attribution is always imperfect. Site search terms, which show what people can't find. And form analytics, which show the fields that cause abandonment. Each metric answers a different question, so start with your question.
```

Scene 4 — Useful tracking in GA4

```text
Setting up useful tracking. Define your conversion events, like form submitted, purchase or booking confirmed. In GA4, you mark these as key events. Then define micro-conversions, like call to action clicked, pricing viewed or video played. Name events consistently. GA4's recommended event generate lead is a good choice for lead forms. Tag your campaign links with UTM parameters, so you know which ad or post drove the visit. And respect privacy. Follow consent requirements for cookies and tracking in the markets you serve, never send personal data like emails or phone numbers in events, and document what you collect.
```

Scene 5 — Heatmaps and recordings

```text
Heatmaps and session recordings. Behavior analytics tools like Microsoft Clarity, Hotjar or Contentsquare show click maps, including clicks on things that aren't clickable, which signals a false affordance. Scroll maps show how far people get. And session recordings show individual journeys, where you'll spot rage clicks and people going back and forth in confusion. Use them responsibly. Mask all form inputs and sensitive text. Disclose use in your privacy notice. Get consent where it's required. And keep recordings only as long as you need. A simple weekly routine: watch ten recordings of people who dropped off.
```

Scene 6 — A/B testing basics

```text
Now A slash B testing. An A slash B test shows different versions to randomly split groups of visitors and compares outcomes. A good test starts with a written plan. The hypothesis: because of an insight, changing an element to a variant will improve a metric for an audience. A primary metric. Guardrail metrics that must not get worse, like refunds, lead quality or page speed. The audience. A sample size and duration calculated in advance, running in whole weeks. And a decision rule agreed before you look at results. Here's the key idea. The plan protects you from fooling yourself.
```

Scene 7 — Pitfalls and when not to test

```text
A slash B testing pitfalls. Too little traffic: small sites often can't detect small changes reliably, so test bolder changes or use qualitative methods. Stopping early: checking daily and stopping when one version looks ahead inflates false winners. Testing many things at once without the right design. Ignoring segments and seasonality, because results can differ by device, channel, or time, like during Ramadan or a big sale. Optimizing the wrong metric: a manipulative change can lift clicks while increasing refunds. And treating one test as universal truth. Also, don't A slash B test obvious bug fixes or accessibility improvements. Just fix them.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 1: newsletter form

```text
Worked example one, simple. A newsletter landing page. GA4 shows many CTA clicks but fewer form submissions. Clarity recordings show people tapping the email field, getting a letter keyboard without an at sign, and giving up. That's a form bug, not a test question. The team changes the field to type email, so phones show the right keyboard, and monitors before and after, comparing similar weeks and the same traffic sources. Form completion on mobile improves. No A slash B test needed. Some fixes are simply fixes.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example 2: Dubai store (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative numbers. A Dubai e-commerce store sees mobile checkout conversion far below desktop. Recordings show shoppers repeatedly tapping a tiny promo code field, then leaving to search for codes. In usability tests, people say they feel they're missing a discount. The fix: collapse the promo field behind a small have a code link, and show automatic discounts clearly. With enough traffic, they run an A slash B test over three full weeks, pre-registered, with customer service contacts as a guardrail. Mobile checkout completion improves, with no increase in contacts.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: events → funnel → recordings

```text
Watch me do it. I add two events. A CTA click event on every button with a data attribute, and generate lead, fired only from the form's success handler, never on button click. No personal data in either. In GA4 admin, I mark generate lead as a key event. Then I build a funnel exploration: page view on the landing page, CTA click, generate lead. I break it down by device and filter to paid social. Mobile loses most people between click and lead. In Clarity, I filter recordings to mobile, paid social, landing page, and watch ten sessions that dropped off. Three show the same confused behavior on the phone number field.
```

Scene 11 — Sanity-check winners, then combine evidence

```text
When a test tool shows a winner, sanity-check it. Did it run for the planned duration, in whole weeks? Are the two groups similar in size? A big mismatch can mean a setup problem, often called a sample ratio mismatch. Did the guardrails hold? Is the result consistent across major segments like device? If not, re-run it, or treat the result as a hint rather than a fact. And combine evidence in a cycle. Analytics shows where the problem is. Recordings and tests show why. You design a fix, validate it quickly, and launch, as an A slash B test if traffic allows, otherwise with careful before and after monitoring.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Collecting data without clear questions. Tracking without consent where it's required, or sending personal data to analytics. Firing the lead event on button click instead of on success. Running A slash B tests on low traffic and trusting noisy results. Peeking and stopping early. And using analytics to confirm opinions instead of to learn. How do you measure success? Whether you can answer, in minutes, where do mobile visitors from paid social drop off, and what did they do just before? Plus the share of tests with written plans, and how many shipped changes hold their gains after launch.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Analytics shows what happens at scale. Testing shows why. Define key events and micro-conversions, tag your links, and respect consent and privacy. Use heatmaps and recordings responsibly to see behavior. Run A slash B tests with a clear hypothesis, a pre-set sample size, guardrails and a decision rule. Don't test obvious fixes. Just fix them. Try this now. Map the funnel for one conversion path in your analytics. Find the biggest drop-off, watch ten recordings of people who left there, and write either an A slash B test plan, or a before and after plan if your traffic is low.
```

</details>

### 21. Iterating and building a UX habit in your team

Lesson `iterating-and-ux-culture` · module “Testing with users and iterating” · target 9 min · 13 scenes · 1236 words · planned 8:57 (words ≈ 8:57) · status: **to produce**

| Term | Say |
|---|---|
| UX | U X |

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0:00 | 43 | Here's a pattern you'll recognize. A team launches a beautiful new website. Everyone celebrates. Then nobody touches it for two years, while customers change, competitors improve, and small problems pile up. Finally, the site feels so outdated that the only option is another expensive redesign. And the cycle repeats. There's a better way. In this final lecture, you'll learn the continuous improvement loop, how to prioritize with impact, confidence and effort, a realistic UX rhythm for small teams, how to document what you learn, how to persuade stakeholders with evidence, and how to keep ethics at the center of optimization. | Iterating and building a UX habit<br>• Launch, neglect, redesign, repeat<br>• A continuous loop instead<br>• Prioritize, document, persuade<br>• Ethics in optimization | Layout: cycle of launch and decay. 0-11s: a shiny website launches with confetti; 11-22s: a calendar flips through two years as small cracks appear; 22-32s: a wrecking ball labeled 'Redesign' swings; 32-43s: the scene resets into a smooth loop diagram as the title appears. |
| 2 | 0:43 | 37 | Why does this matter? Because great experiences are rarely designed in one go. They're improved, week after week. Think of it like fitness. A two-week intense program followed by months of nothing doesn't make you fit. Thirty minutes, three times a week, for a year, does. UX works the same way. Small, regular improvements based on evidence compound into a page that converts better, frustrates fewer people and needs fewer expensive redesigns. And for small teams, a steady rhythm is far more realistic than big projects. | Why it matters<br>• Improved, not designed once<br>• The fitness analogy<br>• Small regular gains compound<br>• A rhythm beats big projects | Layout: two progress lines compared. 0-9s: a spiky line shows a burst of effort then decline; 9-18s: a steady line climbs gradually over twelve months; 18-28s: the steady line overtakes the spiky one; 28-37s: caption: 'Small, regular, compounding'. |
| 3 | 1:20 | 38 | Here's the loop. One, observe: analytics, recordings, feedback, support tickets and tests. Two, prioritize: impact times confidence divided by effort. Three, design: sketch, wireframe, prototype and write copy. Four, validate: a quick usability test or expert review. Five, ship: launch, as an A slash B test if traffic allows. Six, measure: primary and guardrail metrics. Seven, share: document and communicate what you learned. Then back to observe. Here's the key idea. Step seven is the one most teams skip, and it's the one that stops you repeating mistakes. | The continuous improvement loop<br>• Observe → Prioritize → Design<br>• Validate → Ship → Measure<br>• Share (the step most teams skip) | Layout: seven-step circular loop. 0-10s: seven nodes appear around a circle; 10-19s: an arrow travels from Observe to Measure; 19-28s: the Share node pulses brightly; 28-38s: the arrow completes the loop back to Observe. |
| 4 | 1:58 | 40 | Prioritizing. A simple scoring model helps. Rate each idea from one to five for impact, confidence and effort. Then score equals impact times confidence, divided by effort. For example, show per-staff pricing: impact five, confidence four, effort two. Score ten. Redesign the entire site: impact four, confidence two, effort five. Score one point six. Fix the mobile call to action contrast: impact three, confidence five, effort one. Score fifteen. Scores are rough guides, not precise truths. But they force an explicit discussion about evidence and effort, instead of whoever speaks loudest winning. | Prioritize: Impact × Confidence ÷ Effort<br>• Per-staff pricing: 5×4÷2 = 10<br>• Full redesign: 4×2÷5 = 1.6<br>• Fix CTA contrast: 3×5÷1 = 15<br>• Rough guide; explicit evidence | Layout: scoring table animating. 0-10s: three idea rows appear; 10-20s: impact, confidence and effort values fill in; 20-30s: scores calculate: 10, 1.6 and 15; 30-40s: rows reorder with 'Fix CTA contrast' on top. |
| 5 | 2:38 | 43 | Where does confidence come from? Evidence. A one means it's an opinion. A three means one source supports it, like analytics alone. A five means multiple sources agree: analytics, recordings and usability tests all point the same way. That definition matters, because it turns confidence from a feeling into something you can check. Keep your ideas in a UX backlog, one row per idea, with a link to the evidence, the three scores, an owner, a status, and a result column you fill in after launch. The result column is how you learn which kinds of ideas actually work. | Confidence comes from evidence<br>• 1 = opinion<br>• 3 = one source<br>• 5 = several sources agree<br>• Backlog with evidence links and results | Layout: confidence scale and a backlog table. 0-11s: a scale from 1 to 5 appears with labels; 11-22s: evidence icons stack up as the scale rises; 22-32s: a backlog table appears with an 'Evidence' link column; 32-43s: a 'Result' column fills in for a shipped item. |
| 6 | 3:21 | 38 | A realistic UX rhythm for small teams. Weekly, thirty minutes or less: review key funnel metrics and a handful of recordings or customer messages. Monthly: one small usability test round, three to five people, on your highest-priority flow. Quarterly: a heuristic review and accessibility check of your main templates, and a backlog refresh. And every campaign: a message match and mobile QA check before launch, and a review of landing page performance afterward. None of these needs a UX department. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Iterating and building a UX habit

```text
Here's a pattern you'll recognize. A team launches a beautiful new website. Everyone celebrates. Then nobody touches it for two years, while customers change, competitors improve, and small problems pile up. Finally, the site feels so outdated that the only option is another expensive redesign. And the cycle repeats. There's a better way. In this final lecture, you'll learn the continuous improvement loop, how to prioritize with impact, confidence and effort, a realistic UX rhythm for small teams, how to document what you learn, how to persuade stakeholders with evidence, and how to keep ethics at the center of optimization.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because great experiences are rarely designed in one go. They're improved, week after week. Think of it like fitness. A two-week intense program followed by months of nothing doesn't make you fit. Thirty minutes, three times a week, for a year, does. UX works the same way. Small, regular improvements based on evidence compound into a page that converts better, frustrates fewer people and needs fewer expensive redesigns. And for small teams, a steady rhythm is far more realistic than big projects.
```

Scene 3 — The continuous improvement loop

```text
Here's the loop. One, observe: analytics, recordings, feedback, support tickets and tests. Two, prioritize: impact times confidence divided by effort. Three, design: sketch, wireframe, prototype and write copy. Four, validate: a quick usability test or expert review. Five, ship: launch, as an A slash B test if traffic allows. Six, measure: primary and guardrail metrics. Seven, share: document and communicate what you learned. Then back to observe. Here's the key idea. Step seven is the one most teams skip, and it's the one that stops you repeating mistakes.
```

Scene 4 — Prioritize: Impact × Confidence ÷ Effort

```text
Prioritizing. A simple scoring model helps. Rate each idea from one to five for impact, confidence and effort. Then score equals impact times confidence, divided by effort. For example, show per-staff pricing: impact five, confidence four, effort two. Score ten. Redesign the entire site: impact four, confidence two, effort five. Score one point six. Fix the mobile call to action contrast: impact three, confidence five, effort one. Score fifteen. Scores are rough guides, not precise truths. But they force an explicit discussion about evidence and effort, instead of whoever speaks loudest winning.
```

Scene 5 — Confidence comes from evidence

```text
Where does confidence come from? Evidence. A one means it's an opinion. A three means one source supports it, like analytics alone. A five means multiple sources agree: analytics, recordings and usability tests all point the same way. That definition matters, because it turns confidence from a feeling into something you can check. Keep your ideas in a UX backlog, one row per idea, with a link to the evidence, the three scores, an owner, a status, and a result column you fill in after launch. The result column is how you learn which kinds of ideas actually work.
```

Scene 6 — A realistic rhythm

```text
A realistic UX rhythm for small teams. Weekly, thirty minutes or less: review key funnel metrics and a handful of recordings or customer messages. Monthly: one small usability test round, three to five people, on your highest-priority flow. Quarterly: a heuristic review and accessibility check of your main templates, and a backlog refresh. And every campaign: a message match and mobile QA check before launch, and a review of landing page performance afterward. None of these needs a UX department. They need a calendar invite and a habit.
```

Scene 7 — The insight log

```text
Documenting learnings. Keep an insight log, so knowledge doesn't live only in people's heads. Each entry has a date, the source, the insight, the evidence, the action taken, the result, and the next step. For example. Source: five usability tests plus analytics. Insight: parents don't understand the difference between plan A and plan B. Evidence: four of five hesitated, and many pricing visitors toggled plans repeatedly. Action: a comparison table and a which plan is right for me quiz. Result: fewer pre-sale questions, still monitoring. Next: test the quiz copy in the Urdu version.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 1: bakery weekly review

```text
Worked example one, simple. A freelance marketer running a small UX habit for a local bakery's ordering site. Every Monday, twenty minutes: she checks the funnel, watches five recordings and reads five customer messages. One week, three messages ask whether same-day orders are possible. The recordings show people scrolling up and down the product page looking for it. She scores the idea: impact four, confidence four, effort one. Score sixteen. She adds a same-day cut-off time beside the order button. The next week, those questions stop, and she logs the result.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example 2: 90-day program (illustrative)

```text
Worked example two, a business scenario with illustrative details. An agency runs a ninety-day UX program for a clinic's booking site. Month one: a heuristic review, a funnel analysis, and five usability tests on the booking flow. The top five issues get fixed. Month two: with enough traffic, they A slash B test a simplified form, fix accessibility issues in the templates, and start an insight log. Month three: a second test round confirms improvements. The quarterly report shows better booking completion and fewer support questions, and it honestly notes the seasonal demand and a new ad campaign that may also have contributed.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: backlog + monthly summary

```text
Watch me do it. I open our backlog spreadsheet and add a score column with the formula impact times confidence divided by effort, rounded to one decimal. I re-score three items, because this week's recordings added evidence to one of them, so its confidence goes from three to five. It jumps to the top. Next, I paste the month's insight log and funnel table into an approved AI assistant and ask for a one-page founder summary: three wins, three open problems, and next month's plan, using only the data provided and noting other activities that may have affected the numbers. Then I check every number against the source before I send it.
```

Scene 11 — Persuade with evidence; protect ethics

```text
Presenting findings and keeping ethics in the loop. Stakeholders are persuaded by users more than by designers' opinions. So show, don't tell. Short clips of users struggling are powerful, with consent. Connect each issue to a business goal. Be specific: issue, evidence, recommendation, expected impact and effort. Include what's working, so the team knows what to protect. Keep it to one page. And protect ethics. Use guardrail metrics for complaints, refunds, cancellations and accessibility. Set a firm rule that no dark pattern ships, whatever a test says. And include diverse participants: different ages, abilities, languages and devices.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes and measures

```text
Common mistakes. Treating launch as the end of the work. Prioritizing by the loudest opinion instead of evidence. Not documenting learnings, so the same mistakes repeat. Letting metric chasing lead to dark patterns. And sending AI-drafted reports without checking the numbers. How do you measure success? Track the loop itself: how many improvements ship each month, the share backed by evidence, the share that held their gains after launch, and the time from insight to shipped change. Over a quarter, a healthy loop shows steady improvements on your key flows.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap the whole course, and this lesson. UX is a loop: observe, prioritize, design, validate, ship, measure and share. Prioritize with impact times confidence divided by effort, where confidence comes from evidence. Keep a realistic weekly, monthly and quarterly rhythm. Document insights, present findings with user evidence tied to business goals, and keep ethics and inclusion at the center. Try this now. Create an insight log with at least three entries from your recent work. Score five improvement ideas with the formula. And put your team's weekly UX review in the calendar, starting this week.
```

</details>
